The previous PoC design must be corrected to use the standardized RTP timestamp metadata and derive WebCodecs microsecond PTS locally. The newer W3C microsecond metadata field exists in the draft but Chromium's current implementation gates it behind an experimental feature. This is a concrete cross-browser compatibility requirement, not a blocker: RTP timestamp unwrapping is sufficient for a source-local timeline, subject to correct wrap/discontinuity handling. No architecture is selected. Overall status remains **CONDITIONAL GO for isolated PoCs only; NO-GO for architecture lock/full implementation**.

### Evidence

- Chromium runtime feature definitions: https://chromium.googlesource.com/chromium/src/+/main/third_party/blink/renderer/platform/runtime_enabled_features.json5
- Chromium `RTCEncodedVideoFrame::getMetadata()` implementation: https://chromium.googlesource.com/chromium/src/+/main/third_party/blink/renderer/modules/peerconnection/rtc_encoded_video_frame.cc
- MDN `RTCEncodedVideoFrame.getMetadata()`: https://developer.mozilla.org/en-US/docs/Web/API/RTCEncodedVideoFrame/getMetadata
- MDN deprecated/non-standard `RTCEncodedVideoFrame.timestamp`: https://developer.mozilla.org/en-US/docs/Web/API/RTCEncodedVideoFrame/timestamp
- W3C WebRTC Encoded Transform: https://www.w3.org/TR/webrtc-encoded-transform/
## 2026-11-15 — Compressed H.264 ring as authoritative history; decoded cache as best-effort hot window

**Scope:** Synthesis of WebRTC Encoded Transform and WebCodecs queue/resource semantics. This is a buffer-policy candidate, not an architecture decision; no runtime PoC or local changes.

### Design principle

Treat the bounded compressed H.264 access-unit ring as the authoritative recent history, and treat continuous decoding as a best-effort hot cache. A decoder backlog or decoder-resource stall must not destroy the encoded history or block the live view/recording path.

### Candidate worker policy

1. In the dedicated replay receiver transform, construct an immutable `EncodedVideoChunk` from each received complete H.264 access unit. Where supported, pass the data ArrayBuffer in the `transfer` list so the user agent MAY reference the transferred bytes rather than copying them; do not assume zero-copy.
2. Retain each chunk and its metadata in a bounded compressed ring, including source epoch, unwrapped RTP PTS, key/delta type, NAL/access-unit integrity, SPS/PPS identity/version and discontinuity markers. Evict only at a safe keyframe boundary so every retained delta chunk has its decode dependencies.
3. Feed the same immutable EncodedVideoChunk object to the warm WebCodecs decoder. The object is not consumed or detached by `decode()`; the ring can retain the chunk for later pre-roll decode, subject to actual browser memory behaviour and runtime tests.
4. Maintain a bounded decoded hot window. Close every output VideoFrame as soon as it is no longer required. Keep only the selected frame and a short nearby window when paused/reviewing.
5. Track both `decodeQueueSize` and an application-level input/output lag metric. `decodeQueueSize` is the count of pending decode requests and decreases when the underlying codec is ready to accept work; it does not alone describe every frame still in the decoder's internal pipeline. Compare submitted chunk sequence/PTS with output sequence/PTS and elapsed media time.
6. If the warm decoder falls behind, preserve all encoded chunks in the compressed ring. Stop submitting dependent delta chunks to the warm decoder once a gap has occurred; mark the decoded hot window discontinuous and resume warm decoding at the next verified IDR with all required SPS/PPS. Do not drop a delta chunk from the compressed history just because the warm decoder is saturated.
7. For an older replay target outside the decoded hot window, create a seek operation from the nearest preceding IDR in the compressed ring, feed chunks in decode order, discard/close outputs before the requested PTS, then display the target and build a short following window. Measure first-display and target-frame latency.
8. If the worker itself cannot copy/retain encoded access units quickly enough, mark the encoded ring as incomplete and expose the gap. The system must never claim a complete replay interval when access units were dropped.

### Important queue and recovery details

- WebRTC encoded-transform backpressure is disabled. The transform must not await an unbounded decode backlog and assume upstream media will pause safely.
- `VideoDecoder.decodeQueueSize` should be used with `ondequeue`, but application-level output lag and dropped access-unit counters are also required.
- If the warm decoder misses one dependent delta frame, later delta frames may reference it. Resume only from a valid random-access point; do not resume at an arbitrary next frame.
- The compressed ring is still subject to the H.264 Annex B/IDR/SPS/PPS requirements, MediaMTX v1.21.3's B-frame restriction, multi-PPS concerns, source timestamp wrap and reconnect epochs.
- The same EncodedVideoChunk may be retained and submitted to a decoder without mutating it because chunks are immutable; test repeated submission to a new/reused decoder on supported browser implementations.

### PoC comparison

Compare a full decoded cache, a compressed ring plus 0.5–1-second warm decoded window, and a compressed ring with no warm decode but preconfigured on-demand seek decoder. Force decoder saturation and delayed output to verify that encoded history remains complete, hot-cache gaps are labelled, and recovery starts at the next IDR. Measure memory, queue depth, input/output lag, decode throughput, first-display latency, exact frame-step, live-view latency and recording continuity for two cameras.

### Assessment

This policy separates data preservation from decode performance. It may provide a much smaller and more robust history than a full decoded cache while keeping common recent replay positions immediately available. Its success depends on measured GOP length and on-demand pre-roll speed; it is not yet proven to meet the immediate-replay requirement for every position. No architecture is selected. Overall status remains **CONDITIONAL GO for isolated PoCs only; NO-GO for architecture lock/full implementation**.

### Evidence

- W3C WebRTC Encoded Transform backpressure and drop/order rules: https://www.w3.org/TR/webrtc-encoded-transform/
- W3C WebCodecs EncodedVideoChunk immutability/transfer and decoder queue semantics: https://www.w3.org/TR/webcodecs/
- MDN WebCodecs decoder queue management: https://developer.mozilla.org/en-US/docs/Web/API/WebCodecs_API/Using_the_WebCodecs_API
## 2026-11-15 — H.264 RTP timestamp wrap duration and long-event timeline test

**Scope:** Calculation from the H.264 RTP timestamp format defined in RFC 6184. No runtime PoC or local changes.

### Verified timing facts

- RFC 6184 specifies a 32-bit RTP timestamp for H.264 and mandates a 90 kHz clock rate. The timestamp represents the sampling instant of the content.
- A 32-bit 90 kHz clock wraps after `2^32 / 90000` seconds = **47,721.8588 seconds**, approximately **13 hours, 15 minutes, 21.86 seconds**.
- This is long enough to occur during an extended tournament day or a continuously running venue installation. A timestamp implementation that only compares raw unsigned 32-bit values will eventually see a large backwards jump and can corrupt ordering, durations, replay seek and bookmark mapping.

### JudoReplayKit requirements

1. Maintain a 64-bit unwrapped RTP tick counter per camera/source epoch. Use modulo-aware deltas and distinguish a normal 32-bit wrap from a source restart/discontinuity.
2. Keep the raw 32-bit RTP value for diagnostics and the unwrapped 64-bit value for timeline/index operations.
3. Convert to WebCodecs microsecond PTS using the 90 kHz rational clock; avoid accumulating per-frame floating-point increments.
4. Add a synthetic wrap test crossing `0xFFFFFFFF -> 0x00000000` and a long-duration test spanning more than 13h15m of simulated source time.
5. A camera reconnect starts a new source epoch even if the first new RTP timestamp happens to be numerically close to the previous epoch.

### Evidence

- RFC 6184, H.264 RTP timestamp field and 90 kHz clock rate: https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc6184.html
## 2026-11-15 — Exact displayed-frame bookmark candidate via requestVideoFrameCallback RTP/NTP metadata

**Scope:** Review of current MDN/WICG `requestVideoFrameCallback()` WebRTC metadata, Chromium's exact implementation path, Web Platform Test expectations, and MediaMTX v1.21.3 RTP/NTP timestamp generation. Source-level analysis only; no runtime PoC or local changes.

### Browser-side findings

- `HTMLVideoElement.requestVideoFrameCallback()` metadata can include `rtpTimestamp`, `receiveTime` and `captureTime` for WebRTC video. `rtpTimestamp` identifies the RTP timestamp of the actual frame sent to the compositor; `mediaTime` is the presentation timestamp of that frame on the video element timeline; `expectedDisplayTime` indicates when the browser expects it to be visible; `presentedFrames` can detect missed callback frames.
- The WICG Video Frame Request Callback specification says `captureTime` for a remote WebRTC frame is estimated from the RTP timestamp using clock synchronization, with RTCP Sender Reports as one possible method. The metadata is best effort and may be absent if the user agent cannot surface it.
- Chromium's current implementation (`media_stream_remote_video_source.cc`) copies the incoming WebRTC frame's RTP timestamp into the video frame metadata and sets capture-begin time from the incoming frame's NTP time plus the WebRTC clock offset. `video_frame_callback_requester_impl.cc` exposes RTP timestamp, receive time and capture time through `requestVideoFrameCallback()` metadata.
- Chromium's WPT for WebRTC `requestVideoFrameCallback()` asserts that `rtpTimestamp` and `receiveTime` are present, and waits for `captureTime` to appear after RTCP round-trip/clock estimation (or earlier if already present). Therefore `captureTime` may be unavailable initially; feature-detect it and do not make initial bookmark capture depend on it.
- `requestVideoFrameCallback()` callbacks run on the main thread and are not guaranteed to execute synchronously with the compositor; the specification warns callbacks can be delayed by a vsync. The metadata still identifies the frame associated with the callback, but the app must track `presentedFrames`, `expectedDisplayTime` and callback freshness when a bookmark is clicked. Exact click-to-displayed-frame association must be tested under UI load.

### MediaMTX v1.21.3 source-level timestamp correlation

- RTMP publishers are registered with `UseRTPPackets: false` and `ReplaceNTP: true` in `internal/servers/rtmp/conn.go`.
- `internal/stream/sub_stream_format.go` generates RTP packets from the H.264 access unit and sets their timestamp to `rtpTimeOffset + uint32(u.PTS)`. The random RTP offset is held by the stream format; all readers of that format receive units based on the same timestamp base.
- `internal/stream/stream.go` writes those same `u.RTPPackets` to RTSP using `WritePacketRTPWithNTP(..., u.NTP)`. The gortsplib server sends the supplied absolute NTP time in periodic RTCP Sender Reports.
- `internal/protocols/webrtc/from_stream.go` re-packetizes H.264 for each WebRTC reader and adds `u.RTPPackets[0].Timestamp` to the packetizer timestamp. The gortsplib H.264 RTP encoder creates packet headers without setting a timestamp, so its initial timestamp is zero; the resulting WebRTC RTP timestamp is therefore the same base `u.RTPPackets[0].Timestamp` used by the RTSP path for that H.264 access unit.
- The same WebRTC path calls `WriteRTPWithNTP` with `u.NTP` (plus any packetizer-relative timestamp delta). The RTCP Sender Report therefore maps the WebRTC RTP timestamp to the same MediaMTX NTP time that the recorder receives.
- For RTMP input, `internal/ntpestimator/estimator.go` anchors the first PTS to `time.Now()` and estimates subsequent NTP from PTS. It reanchors if the computed time is in the future or more than 5 seconds behind the current system time. This NTP is a MediaMTX/server-estimated timeline, not a guaranteed physical camera-capture clock. It is nevertheless the timebase used by MediaMTX recording and WebRTC Sender Reports.
- `internal/recorder/format_fmp4_track.go` stores `sample.ntp` as segment start NTP and records the matching DTS origin; it checks drift between NTP elapsed time and recording DTS elapsed time, with a 5-second tolerance before resetting the recording timeline.

### Proposed bookmark flow

1. Keep the normal live-view PeerConnection separate from the replay-only PeerConnection/encoded ring. Do not add a heavy application transform to the live path.
2. Run `requestVideoFrameCallback()` continuously on the visible live `<video>` element. On each callback, retain a compact record containing camera/source epoch, `rtpTimestamp`, `captureTime` if present, `receiveTime`, `mediaTime`, `presentedFrames`, `expectedDisplayTime` and callback time.
3. When the referee creates a bookmark, bind it to the latest valid displayed-frame metadata, not `Date.now()` and not the newest frame received by the replay reader. This accounts for configured live delay and the browser's jitter/playout buffer because the RTP timestamp belongs to the frame actually sent to the compositor.
4. Match that raw per-camera RTP timestamp against the replay worker's compressed ring. MediaMTX v1.21.3 source indicates that its RTSP and WebRTC readers for the same path/stream format share the same RTP timestamp base. Validate this across separate PeerConnections and camera reconnects before relying on it.
5. For archive lookup, use `captureTime` as a candidate server-NTP mapping once it is present and validated. Since MediaMTX's RTMP NTP estimator uses the server clock and the fMP4 recorder uses the same `u.NTP`, this should align the bookmark with the recording timeline. Store the raw RTP timestamp and source epoch as the primary identity; store captureTime/timeOrigin as an absolute-time aid.
6. If `captureTime` is absent or unreliable, use a local RTSP metadata sidecar as fallback/validation. The gortsplib v5.6.7 dependency used by MediaMTX v1.21.3 provides `Client.PacketNTP(medi, pkt)`, which computes NTP from incoming RTCP Sender Reports. A sidecar can publish `(RTP timestamp, NTP, camera path, epoch)` mapping updates without modifying the MediaMTX recording path.
7. Compare browser `captureTime` and sidecar `PacketNTP` for matching RTP timestamps over long runs, camera reconnects and NTP estimator reanchors. Record the difference and uncertainty; do not claim true camera capture time or cross-camera synchronization from this alone.

### Important limitations

- `captureTime` is estimated from RTCP Sender Reports and MediaMTX's NTP estimator. For RTMP camera input, this maps to the MediaMTX server-estimated timeline, not necessarily the phone's physical sensor exposure time. This is suitable for matching the bookmark to MediaMTX's own archive if validated, but not proof of true capture-time synchronization between two phones.
- The browser callback may be delayed relative to the compositor by up to a vsync; track `presentedFrames` and `expectedDisplayTime`, and test bookmark click timing. If the callback is stale or metadata is absent, mark the bookmark time quality/uncertainty rather than silently substituting wall-clock time.
- Raw RTP timestamps are 32-bit and wrap. Maintain source epochs and wrap-safe mapping; reconnects and MediaMTX stream-format restarts must start a new epoch.
- The RTP timestamp equivalence between MediaMTX RTSP and WebRTC is source-code-derived for the same path and H.264 stream format. Runtime verification is mandatory, particularly if multiple H.264 formats/profiles are negotiated or MediaMTX changes its packetization implementation.
- The RTSP sidecar would add a local RTSP reader and loopback traffic. It is a fallback/validation tool, not automatically required if browser captureTime proves accurate and stable.

### Required PoC tests

1. In Chrome and Edge on Windows and Linux, log actual `requestVideoFrameCallback` metadata for remote MediaMTX WebRTC video. Verify `rtpTimestamp`, `receiveTime`, `captureTime`, `mediaTime`, `presentedFrames` and `expectedDisplayTime` presence and semantics.
2. Compare the live `<video>` callback's `rtpTimestamp` against the replay-only receiver worker's `RTCEncodedVideoFrame.getMetadata().rtpTimestamp` for the same displayed frame. Verify exact matching across separate WebRTC PeerConnections.
3. Create bookmarks at randomized times, including during fast motion and under UI load. Confirm the selected RTP timestamp identifies the frame actually visible at click time, accounting for callback/vsync timing.
4. Compare `captureTime` converted through `performance.timeOrigin` with RTSP sidecar `PacketNTP` for the same RTP timestamps. Measure offset, jitter and drift over long sessions.
5. Repeat across MediaMTX restart, RTMP reconnect, source PTS discontinuity, 32-bit RTP wrap, NTP estimator reanchor and two-camera operation.
6. Verify that the resulting bookmark NTP selects the correct fMP4 segment and exact media time; compare extracted frame identity/visual content at the bookmark against the live frame.

### Assessment

This is a major improvement to bookmark design. `requestVideoFrameCallback()` can identify the actual frame being presented, including its RTP timestamp, and Chromium can expose an RTCP-SR-derived captureTime. MediaMTX v1.21.3 source indicates that the same RTP timestamp base is used across its RTSP and WebRTC output for the same H.264 stream format, while the NTP reference is shared with recording. The RTSP sidecar remains a useful fallback and independent validation path. This could avoid modifying MediaMTX and avoid adding any heavy processing to the live PeerConnection. However, browser metadata presence, callback freshness, cross-connection timestamp equality and archive alignment still require a runtime PoC. No architecture is selected. Overall status remains **CONDITIONAL GO for isolated PoCs only; NO-GO for architecture lock/full implementation**.

### Evidence

- MDN `requestVideoFrameCallback()` and WebRTC frame metadata: https://developer.mozilla.org/en-US/docs/Web/API/HTMLVideoElement/requestVideoFrameCallback
- WICG Video Frame Request Callback specification: https://wicg.github.io/video-rvfc/
- Chromium remote WebRTC video frame metadata: https://chromium.googlesource.com/chromium/src/+/master/third_party/blink/renderer/modules/peerconnection/media_stream_remote_video_source.cc
- Chromium mapping of metadata to JS callback: https://chromium.googlesource.com/chromium/src/+/main/third_party/blink/renderer/modules/video_rvfc/video_frame_callback_requester_impl.cc
- Chromium WebRTC requestVideoFrameCallback WPT: https://chromium.googlesource.com/chromium/src/+/main/third_party/blink/web_tests/external/wpt/video-rvfc/request-video-frame-callback-webrtc.https.html
- MediaMTX v1.21.3 RTMP publisher timestamp configuration: https://github.com/bluenviron/mediamtx/blob/v1.21.3/internal/servers/rtmp/conn.go
- MediaMTX v1.21.3 RTP timestamp generation and RTSP write: https://github.com/bluenviron/mediamtx/blob/v1.21.3/internal/stream/sub_stream_format.go
- MediaMTX v1.21.3 WebRTC H.264 RTP timestamp mapping: https://github.com/bluenviron/mediamtx/blob/v1.21.3/internal/protocols/webrtc/from_stream.go
- MediaMTX v1.21.3 NTP estimator: https://github.com/bluenviron/mediamtx/blob/v1.21.3/internal/ntpestimator/estimator.go
- MediaMTX v1.21.3 fMP4 timestamp/segment mapping: https://github.com/bluenviron/mediamtx/blob/v1.21.3/internal/recorder/format_fmp4_track.go
- gortsplib v5.6.7 RTCP SR to RTP/NTP mapping (`Client.PacketNTP`): https://github.com/bluenviron/gortsplib/blob/v5.6.7/client.go
## 2026-11-15 — Rev.9 two-camera bookmark semantics: shared event time versus per-camera media positions

**Scope:** Cross-check of the owner-confirmed Rev.9 Requirements Draft 0.4 against the new displayed-frame timestamp analysis. Requirements interpretation and design note only; no owner approval is inferred and no implementation is authorized.

### Binding requirements recalled from Rev.9 draft 0.4

- One bookmark represents one shared event/reference moment.
- Store the shared event/reference timestamp and the corresponding mapped media position for each camera.
- When opening a bookmark, show both camera views where each camera's recording covers the event. The two views do not have to be perfectly identical in time; mapping offset/uncertainty must remain honest.
- A bookmark can be created from Instant Replay and points to the media/event timestamp currently viewed, not button-click wall-clock time. Live-time bookmarking remains in scope if practical and must map to the media time represented by the live-delay view.

### Mapping model

1. For a bookmark created from a live camera view, use `requestVideoFrameCallback()` metadata for the frame actually presented by that camera. Its `rtpTimestamp` is a direct per-camera frame identity; `captureTime` is an optional server-NTP-derived time reference; `mediaTime` identifies the presentation timestamp on that video element's own timeline.
2. For a bookmark created while reviewing Instant Replay, use the replay worker's selected frame identity/PTS, not the current live clock or the replay button's wall-clock time.
3. Select one camera/frame as the bookmark's reference event moment. Store that shared event/reference timestamp separately from each camera's mapped media position. Map the reference into the other camera's timeline using the measured per-camera offset and uncertainty.
4. Preserve each camera's raw RTP timestamp and source epoch so that the exact frame can be found in its live ring and mapped to its archive. Do not assume camera 1 and camera 2 RTP timestamps share the same origin or can be directly compared.
5. `captureTime`/RTCP Sender Report mapping from MediaMTX is a server-estimated NTP timeline. For RTMP input, MediaMTX anchors each stream's NTP estimator independently to server time on the first frame and advances it from source PTS. This is suitable for aligning a frame to that camera's MediaMTX recording timeline, but the difference between cameras may include their independent source-to-server latency.
6. Therefore, a common server-NTP event timestamp does not prove true simultaneous sensor capture. Cross-camera mapping must retain measured offset and uncertainty. If automatic offset estimation cannot be validated, an explicit calibration procedure/common visible event is a possible fallback to test, not an approved requirement change.

### Consequences

- The RTSP sidecar proposed above provides an independent mapping from each camera's RTP timestamp to MediaMTX NTP. It improves archive lookup and validation but does not remove camera-to-server latency differences or establish physical capture synchronization.
- `requestVideoFrameCallback()` solves a different problem: it identifies the actual frame presented to the referee, including when Live Delay is active. This avoids substituting the latest received replay frame or the wall-clock button press.
- The same raw RTP timestamp can be used to find the corresponding frame in the replay-only ring only after validating MediaMTX v1.21.3's same-path RTSP/WebRTC timestamp base with separate PeerConnections.
- Store both the shared event reference and the two camera-specific positions. Do not collapse them into one timestamp or force both cameras to show identical frame times.

### Required tests

1. Create bookmarks from Camera 1 live view, Camera 2 live view, and Instant Replay. Verify the selected reference frame and both mapped camera positions.
2. Repeat with Live Delay values 1–5 seconds. The bookmark must point to the frame actually shown, not the newest incoming frame.
3. Compare camera-specific `captureTime`/RTCP NTP against a common visual/audible event visible in both cameras. Quantify residual offset and uncertainty under changing Wi-Fi/network conditions.
4. Test callback freshness and vsync delay under UI load; record `presentedFrames`, `expectedDisplayTime` and the chosen frame RTP timestamp.
5. Reopen bookmarks from the continuous fMP4 archive and verify that each camera lands on the mapped event position, while preserving independent frame stepping.

### Assessment

The displayed-frame RTP metadata can provide exact per-camera frame identity for bookmarks; MediaMTX's NTP mapping can align that frame to its own archive. Cross-camera alignment remains a separate estimation problem because RTMP input's server-estimated NTP anchor is independent per camera and includes source-to-server latency. This distinction matches Rev.9's requirement to store one shared event reference plus per-camera positions and report offset/uncertainty honestly. No architecture is selected and no implementation is authorized.

### Evidence

- Rev.9 Requirements Draft 0.4, sections 4, 7 and owner clarifications: https://github.com/Hotvald/JudoReplayKit/blob/main/REV9-REQUIREMENTS-DRAFT.md
- MediaMTX v1.21.3 RTMP publisher sets `ReplaceNTP: true`: https://github.com/bluenviron/mediamtx/blob/v1.21.3/internal/servers/rtmp/conn.go
- MediaMTX v1.21.3 NTP estimator anchors each stream independently: https://github.com/bluenviron/mediamtx/blob/v1.21.3/internal/ntpestimator/estimator.go
- WICG Video Frame Request Callback WebRTC metadata: https://wicg.github.io/video-rvfc/
## 2026-11-15 — MediaMTX WebRTC RTCP Sender Report cadence and NTP estimator drift

**Scope:** Exact source inspection of MediaMTX v1.21.3 `internal/protocols/webrtc/outbound_track.go`, `internal/ntpestimator/estimator.go`, and recorder drift tolerance. No runtime PoC or local changes.

### Verified findings

- MediaMTX v1.21.3 creates a `rtpsender.Sender` for each WebRTC outbound track with `Period: 1 * time.Second`. The sender generates periodic RTCP Sender Reports using the last RTP timestamp/NTP pair and extrapolates both using elapsed local system time.
- `OutboundTrack.WriteRTPWithNTP()` sets the packet SSRC, calls `rtcpSender.ProcessPacket(pkt, ntp, true)`, then writes the RTP packet. Thus the RTCP sender learns the exact RTP timestamp and NTP pair supplied for the media packet.
- For H.264, the WebRTC output path supplies `u.NTP` plus the packetizer-relative timestamp delta and adds the shared MediaMTX stream-format RTP timestamp base. The RTCP Sender Report therefore carries the mapping needed by the receiver to estimate the same MediaMTX NTP time used by the recorder.
- Chromium's remote WebRTC video source converts the received frame's NTP time (derived from RTCP Sender Reports) into local capture-time metadata. `requestVideoFrameCallback()` then exposes this as `captureTime` when available. The WPT indicates that captureTime may not be present until clock/round-trip estimation has completed; raw `rtpTimestamp` and `receiveTime` are available earlier.
- MediaMTX's RTMP path sets `ReplaceNTP: true`. Its `ntpestimator.Estimate()` anchors the first PTS to `time.Now()` and then calculates NTP from the PTS delta at the configured clock rate. It reanchors if the computed timestamp is in the future or more than 5 seconds behind current system time.
- The NTP estimator does not continuously estimate and correct small source clock-rate drift. A small rate mismatch can therefore accumulate into a growing offset until a reanchor condition is reached. The fMP4 recorder separately detects drift between sample NTP elapsed time and DTS elapsed time using `ntpDriftTolerance = 5 seconds`; when exceeded, it resets the recording timeline.

### Consequences for JudoReplayKit

1. The primary Chrome/Edge bookmark path can likely use `requestVideoFrameCallback()` `rtpTimestamp` immediately to identify the exact displayed frame, then use `captureTime` when the browser's RTCP/SR clock mapping is available to locate that frame on the MediaMTX archive timeline.
2. The 1-second RTCP SR cadence makes this mapping practical without waiting for a segment to close. It does not guarantee captureTime will be present immediately after a new WebRTC connection; keep raw RTP identity and defer NTP resolution or use the sidecar fallback.
3. An RTSP sidecar using gortsplib `PacketNTP()` remains useful as an independent validation/fallback path. Compare its NTP for a given RTP timestamp with browser `captureTime` converted to the same clock domain.
4. Store per-camera source epochs and monitor NTP-vs-PTS drift over long tournament sessions. A camera reconnect or MediaMTX reanchor must create a detectable discontinuity, not silently shift old bookmarks.
5. Cross-camera alignment still cannot be claimed as physical capture synchronization for RTMP sources: each stream's NTP estimator is anchored independently to the server's first-arrival time. The 1-second Sender Report cadence accurately distributes that server-estimated mapping; it does not remove source-to-server latency differences.
6. Long-duration tests must compare both cameras' per-camera PTS-to-NTP slope and offset. Report measured offset and uncertainty, and detect drift before it reaches MediaMTX's 5-second recorder reset tolerance.

### Required PoC additions

- Verify that browser `captureTime` appears on the actual MediaMTX v1.21.3 WebRTC stream and determine time-to-availability after initial connection and reconnect.
- For the same H.264 access unit, compare browser callback `rtpTimestamp`/`captureTime` with RTSP sidecar `PacketNTP()` mapping. Record offset, jitter and drift over hours.
- Simulate source PTS rate drift, PTS jumps, MediaMTX reanchor and reconnect. Verify bookmarks remain attached to the correct per-camera media frame and any mapping discontinuity is surfaced.
- Verify that the bookmark's `captureTime` corresponds to the fMP4 segment's embedded NTP/DTS mapping, not merely the browser's receive wall clock.

### Assessment

MediaMTX's 1-second RTCP Sender Reports plus Chromium's WebRTC captureTime metadata provide a plausible browser-native RTP-to-archive-NTP mapping. The RTSP sidecar can be retained as a validation/fallback rather than assumed mandatory. The main remaining timing risk is not the RTP/NTP transport mapping within one camera; it is the independent RTMP NTP anchors and possible clock drift between two mobile sources. No architecture is selected. Overall status remains **CONDITIONAL GO for isolated PoCs only; NO-GO for architecture lock/full implementation**.

### Evidence

- MediaMTX v1.21.3 WebRTC outbound track and 1-second RTCP sender cadence: https://github.com/bluenviron/mediamtx/blob/v1.21.3/internal/protocols/webrtc/outbound_track.go
- MediaMTX v1.21.3 RTMP publisher NTP replacement: https://github.com/bluenviron/mediamtx/blob/v1.21.3/internal/servers/rtmp/conn.go
- MediaMTX v1.21.3 NTP estimator: https://github.com/bluenviron/mediamtx/blob/v1.21.3/internal/ntpestimator/estimator.go
- MediaMTX v1.21.3 recorder drift tolerance: https://github.com/bluenviron/mediamtx/blob/v1.21.3/internal/recorder/recorder.go
- Chromium remote WebRTC video frame NTP/captureTime mapping: https://chromium.googlesource.com/chromium/src/+/master/third_party/blink/renderer/modules/peerconnection/media_stream_remote_video_source.cc
- WICG WebRTC requestVideoFrameCallback metadata: https://wicg.github.io/video-rvfc/
## 2026-11-15 — MediaMTX fMP4 H.264 stores PTS offset separately from DTS

**Scope:** Exact source inspection of MediaMTX v1.21.3 `internal/recorder/format_fmp4.go` and `format_fmp4_track.go`. Source-level timestamp mapping finding; no runtime PoC or local changes.

### Verified H.264 recording behaviour

- The fMP4 recorder initializes an H.264 DTS extractor at the first random-access access unit. For each subsequent H.264 access unit, it calls `DTSExtractor.Extract(payload, u.PTS)` to derive DTS.
- The fMP4 sample is written with `PTSOffset = u.PTS - dts`, the derived `dts`, and `ntp = u.NTP`. Thus the recorder preserves presentation/decode offset explicitly; NTP is supplied from the stream unit's PTS-based NTP estimate.
- The fMP4 track records the first sample's NTP and DTS as the segment's `startNTP` and `startDTS`. It checks that elapsed NTP and elapsed DTS remain within the configured 5-second drift tolerance; if the tolerance is exceeded, the track resets its segment/timeline state.
- With a no-B-frame H.264 stream, PTS and DTS should coincide and `PTSOffset` should be zero. With B-frames, PTS and DTS differ; the bookmark's presentation time cannot be converted to MP4 decode time by simply subtracting elapsed time from `startDTS` without accounting for each sample's PTSOffset.
- MediaMTX v1.21.3 WebRTC H.264 output rejects B-frame streams. Therefore a no-B-frame source is already a hard compatibility requirement for the proposed WebRTC replay path and also simplifies exact bookmark-to-fMP4 mapping.

### Consequences for bookmarks and archive seeking

1. Keep the bookmark's presentation timestamp/NTP as the event identity. Do not store only a raw MP4 DTS offset.
2. For the required no-B-frame source, verify the recorder's H.264 `PTSOffset` is zero and the WebRTC RTP timestamp maps to the same PTS/NTP sample. This gives a straightforward archive lookup.
3. If a future source contains B-frames, treat it as a separate unsupported/conditional path: preserve PTSOffset and map to the correct sample presentation time. Do not assume the WebRTC and fMP4 paths support the same bitstream.
4. During the PoC, compare browser `captureTime`/RTP timestamp with the corresponding fMP4 sample's NTP, DTS and PTSOffset. Verify the exact displayed frame can be recovered from the archive after segment rotation/closure.

### Evidence

- MediaMTX v1.21.3 fMP4 H.264 DTS/PTSOffset/NTP sample creation: https://github.com/bluenviron/mediamtx/blob/v1.21.3/internal/recorder/format_fmp4.go
- MediaMTX v1.21.3 fMP4 track startNTP/startDTS and drift validation: https://github.com/bluenviron/mediamtx/blob/v1.21.3/internal/recorder/format_fmp4_track.go
- MediaMTX v1.21.3 WebRTC H.264 B-frame restriction: https://github.com/bluenviron/mediamtx/blob/v1.21.3/internal/protocols/webrtc/from_stream.go
## 2026-11-15 — RTCRtpReceiver.jitterBufferTarget as a Live Delay mechanism

**Scope:** Review of the W3C WebRTC Recommendation, MDN documentation and current browser compatibility data. Candidate mechanism only; no runtime PoC, local changes, or architecture decision.

### Verified API behaviour

- `RTCRtpReceiver.jitterBufferTarget` lets an application specify a preferred jitter-buffer hold duration in milliseconds. The setter accepts values from 0 through 4000 ms; values below zero or above 4000 ms throw RangeError.
- The property is a target/hint, not a direct command to set the actual browser buffer. The user agent clamps the actual target to implementation-defined minimum/maximum values based on network conditions and memory constraints. The property getter returns the requested value, not the actual buffer delay.
- The target influences retransmission and packet-loss recovery as well as playout delay. Changing it takes effect gradually; the W3C specification says it may take several seconds to achieve 1 second of buffering and SHOULD not take more than 30 seconds while packets continue to arrive.
- During adjustment, the user agent may accelerate/decelerate media. For video this can mean repeated frames or dropped frames. Therefore Live Delay changes may not be instantaneous or frame-perfect during the transition.
- If audio and video receivers are synchronized, the user agent SHOULD use the larger of the two receivers' `jitterBufferTarget` values for both receivers. If JudoReplayKit receives synchronized audio and video and uses the target on video, set/measure the audio receiver consistently or explicitly validate the resulting A/V behaviour.
- Current compatibility data reports support in Chrome 124+, Edge 124+ and Firefox 115+; Safari is not currently supported in the reviewed compatibility data. The current user environment's Chrome version is above this threshold, but the feature must still be detected at runtime.

### Measurement and telemetry

- `RTCInboundRtpStreamStats.jitterBufferDelay / jitterBufferEmittedCount` gives the average actual time samples/frames spend in the jitter buffer.
- `jitterBufferTargetDelay / jitterBufferEmittedCount` gives the average target delay applied over emitted samples/frames. `jitterBufferMinimumDelay / jitterBufferEmittedCount` estimates the minimum buffer delay required by network conditions without external target mechanisms.
- These metrics must be read as deltas over a measurement interval, not as absolute instantaneous delay. The difference between average target delay and minimum delay can show how much additional hold the application requested; average `jitterBufferDelay` shows actual buffer residence time.
- `requestVideoFrameCallback()` `captureTime` and `expectedDisplayTime` can provide a separate end-to-end MediaMTX-NTP-to-presentation measurement once captureTime is available. This includes server-estimated source time and all subsequent delivery/playout stages, not guaranteed physical camera sensor capture time.

### JudoReplayKit candidate topology

1. Keep the visible live-view PeerConnection separate from the replay-only PeerConnection and its worker-owned compressed ring.
2. Apply `jitterBufferTarget` only to the live-view video receiver, using the selected Live Delay target where supported. Keep the replay-only receiver at its normal/low-latency setting so the replay ring remains warm near the live edge.
3. Use `requestVideoFrameCallback()` on the live video element to record the actual displayed frame's RTP timestamp and captureTime. A bookmark then refers to the delayed frame actually shown, not the replay reader's newer incoming frame.
4. Measure actual delay with `jitterBufferDelay`, `jitterBufferTargetDelay`, `jitterBufferMinimumDelay`, `captureTime`, `expectedDisplayTime` and `receiveTime`. Do not display the selected target as a guaranteed actual delay.
5. Set the target before the match/live session where possible. If the user changes delay during a match, show/record the transition until the actual buffer reaches a stable range; do not imply immediate exact switching.

### Five-second Live Delay limitation

- The API's maximum requested target is 4 seconds, while Rev.9 draft 0.4 confirms Live Delay choices of 1, 2, 3, 4 and 5 seconds.
- `jitterBufferTarget = 4000` cannot by itself guarantee a 5-second selected delay. Natural source/network/browser delay may make total displayed delay exceed 5 seconds, but it cannot be assumed to fill the missing second.
- A 5-second option may require an additional application-controlled post-decode delay or another media path. A 1-second decoded frame buffer at 1080p30 is a non-trivial but potentially manageable memory cost; retaining hardware VideoFrames may still pin decoder surfaces, so this would need a copied bounded representation and a separate PoC.
- Do not silently map the 5-second selection to 4 seconds. Preserve the UI requirement and test an explicit 4-second jitter target plus a 1-second application delay, or another measured solution.

### Required PoC tests

- Test Live Delay targets 1, 2, 3 and 4 seconds with the actual two-camera MediaMTX WebRTC path; measure time to stabilize, actual jitter-buffer delay, total NTP-to-display delay, repeated/dropped frames, packet loss/retransmissions, CPU/GPU and memory.
- Test the 5-second option separately; compare 4-second jitter target plus a 1-second post-decode buffer against any alternative. Measure the actual displayed delay and bookmark timestamp correctness.
- Confirm replay-only receiver remains unaffected by live receiver target changes and that its encoded ring stays near the live edge.
- If audio is present, test audio/video synchronization when setting the target on both receivers.
- Repeat target changes during active stream and under Wi-Fi jitter/loss. Check whether repeated/dropped frames during adjustment affect user perception or bookmark capture.

### Assessment

`jitterBufferTarget` is a strong standards-based candidate for Live Delay 1–4 seconds on Chrome/Edge/Firefox and keeps the delay control on the live receiver rather than the replay reader. It does not directly implement a guaranteed end-to-end delay, and it cannot express the required 5-second target. The 5-second choice needs a separate bounded delay mechanism or a different measured path. This fits the requirement that Live Delay be independent of Instant Replay but remains a PoC candidate only. No architecture is selected. Overall status remains **CONDITIONAL GO for isolated PoCs only; NO-GO for architecture lock/full implementation**.

### Evidence

- W3C WebRTC `RTCRtpReceiver.jitterBufferTarget` normative algorithm: https://www.w3.org/TR/webrtc/#dom-rtcrtpreceiver-jitterbuffertarget
- W3C WebRTC jitter-buffer target and gradual adjustment: https://www.w3.org/TR/webrtc/#dom-rtcrtpreceiver-jitterbuffertarget
- MDN `RTCRtpReceiver.jitterBufferTarget` range and limitations: https://developer.mozilla.org/en-US/docs/Web/API/RTCRtpReceiver/jitterBufferTarget
- MDN `RTCInboundRtpStreamStats.jitterBufferDelay`: https://developer.mozilla.org/en-US/docs/Web/API/RTCInboundRtpStreamStats/jitterBufferDelay
- W3C WebRTC Stats definitions: https://www.w3.org/TR/webrtc-stats/
- Browser compatibility: https://caniuse.com/mdn-api_rtcrtpreceiver_jitterbuffertarget
## 2026-11-15 — HLS ProgramDateTime and hls.js fragment PTS as an alternative live-frame clock

**Scope:** Exact source inspection of MediaMTX v1.21.3 HLS mapping and gohlslib v2.4.7 playlist generation, plus hls.js fragment/latency APIs. Alternative transport analysis only; no runtime PoC or local changes.

### Verified MediaMTX/gohlslib behaviour

- MediaMTX v1.21.3's HLS output path passes each video access unit to gohlslib with both `u.NTP` and `u.PTS` (`internal/protocols/hls/from_stream.go`).
- gohlslib v2.4.7 stores each HLS segment's `startNTP` and `startDTS`. Its media playlist serializer writes `EXT-X-PROGRAM-DATE-TIME` using `seg.startNTP`.
- For low-latency HLS playlists, gohlslib includes program-date-time on the most recent two segments, which should cover the current live segment and its predecessor. Non-low-latency playlists include the segment date-time values in the normal playlist.
- In LL-HLS, the segment contains partial segments/parts. hls.js represents each part with `start = fragment.start + fragOffset`; the parent fragment retains `programDateTime`.
- MediaMTX's HLS output therefore exposes a server-estimated NTP time on the playlist timeline. This is the same `u.NTP` source used by the fMP4 recorder for that path.

### Verified hls.js mapping APIs

- hls.js `Fragment.programDateTime` is an epoch-millisecond value parsed from `EXT-X-PROGRAM-DATE-TIME`.
- hls.js `Fragment.startPTS` and `endPTS` are populated after transmuxing and represent the actual presentation-time range of the fragment; `startDTS`/`endDTS` are also available separately.
- `Fragment.start` is the fragment's start on the hls.js media timeline. `Part.start` is the parent fragment start plus the part's offset.
- A candidate mapping for a displayed HLS frame is `NTP_ms = frag.programDateTime + (frameMediaTime - frag.startPTS) * 1000`, provided the callback's `mediaTime` is on the same MSE presentation timeline and `startPTS` is the correct video-track start. This must be validated for discontinuities, transmux timestamp offsets, audio/video track alignment and B-frame composition offsets.
- hls.js exposes live latency controls including `liveSyncDuration` in seconds and `liveSyncDurationCount` in target-segment units. Its documentation warns that a target below roughly three segment durations is likely to cause stalls. In low-latency mode, the controller can use manifest `PART-HOLD-BACK` when no explicit target is configured; explicit `liveSyncDuration` takes precedence.
- hls.js may change playback rate to catch up when `maxLiveSyncPlaybackRate` is configured above 1, and it can seek back to `liveSyncPosition` when outside the permitted live latency window. This can cause repeated/dropped frames or jumps during recovery and must be measured for referee use.

### Candidate HLS live + WebRTC replay topology

1. Keep the visible live view on HLS/hls.js if that is the selected or existing live transport. Set its target live delay using hls.js latency controls, while keeping the replay path entirely separate.
2. Use `requestVideoFrameCallback()` on the visible HLS `<video>` element to capture the exact displayed frame's `mediaTime`.
3. Maintain a rolling map of current hls.js fragments/parts, including `programDateTime`, `startPTS`, `endPTS`, `start`, `duration`, discontinuity counter and track-specific PTS ranges.
4. Convert the displayed frame's `mediaTime` to MediaMTX NTP using the containing fragment's programDateTime and actual video startPTS. Store the exact `mediaTime` and fragment identity as well as the derived NTP.
5. The independent WebRTC replay worker can map its encoded RTP timestamps to the same NTP via Chromium `captureTime` or the RTSP/RTCP Sender Report sidecar. It can then locate the exact HLS-bookmarked event in the compressed replay ring.
6. If hls.js metadata does not provide reliable frame-to-PDT mapping, retain the WebRTC live-view `requestVideoFrameCallback` path as the more direct RTP/captureTime alternative.

### Live Delay implications

- hls.js `liveSyncDuration` can request a target in seconds, including 5 seconds; unlike `RTCRtpReceiver.jitterBufferTarget`, it has no 4,000 ms API setter cap.
- This does not mean a 1-second HLS target is reliable. With MediaMTX's default 1-second minimum segment duration, hls.js warns that a target below roughly three segment durations may stall. LL-HLS part holdback may permit a lower target, but it must be tested with the exact MediaMTX playlist and hls.js version.
- MediaMTX v1.21.3 default HLS values documented for this release include 1-second segment duration and 200-ms part duration. The actual playlist's `PART-HOLD-BACK`, target duration and part durations must be inspected rather than inferred from config defaults.
- HLS live latency recovery may change playback rate or jump to the live sync position. For a referee display, any such correction must be visible/telemetried and must not alter the bookmark's selected media timestamp.

### Advantages and risks versus WebRTC live view

**Potential advantages:** HLS ProgramDateTime is already part of MediaMTX output and is directly tied to the recorder's NTP timeline; hls.js exposes fragment programDateTime and actual PTS ranges; live delay can be requested in seconds without the WebRTC jitterBufferTarget 4-second cap; existing HLS live view may be reusable.

**Risks:** HLS/hls.js target latency is not exact; 1–2-second targets may stall depending on part holdback and segment duration; fragment-to-video-frame PTS mapping must be validated; hls.js may alter playback rate or seek on stalls; the replay WebRTC path still has the H.264 no-B-frame and SPS/PPS requirements.

### Required PoC comparison

Compare (A) WebRTC live view + requestVideoFrameCallback RTP/captureTime + separate WebRTC replay worker, and (B) HLS live view + hls.js ProgramDateTime/PTS mapping + separate WebRTC replay worker. For each, measure actual displayed delay for 1–5-second settings, frame drops/repeats, timestamp-to-archive accuracy, bookmark frame identity, recovery jumps, CPU/GPU, memory and live/recording continuity. Cross-check HLS-derived NTP against fMP4 segment NTP and RTSP sidecar PacketNTP for the same source event.

### Assessment

HLS ProgramDateTime is a viable alternative source of absolute MediaMTX timeline for the visible frame, and hls.js exposes enough fragment PTS metadata to attempt exact mapping from `requestVideoFrameCallback.mediaTime`. It may offer a more flexible 5-second Live Delay than WebRTC's 4-second jitterBufferTarget cap. However, short HLS live targets and fragment/frame timestamp mapping are not yet proven. Keep this as a separate transport PoC; do not replace the WebRTC replay worker or declare HLS exact frame stepping without testing. No architecture is selected. Overall status remains **CONDITIONAL GO for isolated PoCs only; NO-GO for architecture lock/full implementation**.

### Evidence

- MediaMTX v1.21.3 HLS output passes `u.NTP` and `u.PTS`: https://github.com/bluenviron/mediamtx/blob/v1.21.3/internal/protocols/hls/from_stream.go
- gohlslib v2.4.7 segment PDT generation and low-latency playlist: https://github.com/bluenviron/gohlslib/blob/v2.4.7/muxer_stream.go
- gohlslib v2.4.7 segment NTP/DTS mapping: https://github.com/bluenviron/gohlslib/blob/v2.4.7/muxer_segmenter.go
- hls.js Fragment `programDateTime`, `startPTS`, `startDTS` and Part offsets: https://github.com/video-dev/hls.js/blob/master/src/loader/fragment.ts
- hls.js live latency controls: https://github.com/video-dev/hls.js/blob/master/docs/API.md
- MediaMTX HLS configuration defaults: https://mediamtx.org/docs/references/configuration-file
## 2026-11-15 — LL-HLS ProgramDateTime coverage and hls.js forward-only propagation

**Scope:** Exact inspection of gohlslib v2.4.7 low-latency playlist serialization and hls.js current `Fragment`, `level-helper` and `m3u8-parser` source. No runtime PoC or local changes.

### Verified details

- gohlslib v2.4.7's low-latency playlist serializer emits `EXT-X-PROGRAM-DATE-TIME` for the most recent two complete segments; older segments in the same playlist may have no explicit PDT tag. The current segment's partial parts are associated with the parent fragment and its program time.
- hls.js `Fragment.programDateTime` is nullable and parsed from `EXT-X-PROGRAM-DATE-TIME`. `Fragment.startPTS`/`endPTS` and `startDTS`/`endDTS` are set after transmux. `Part.start` is parent fragment start plus the accumulated part offset.
- hls.js `updateFragPTSDTS()` sets the fragment's `startPTS` to the minimum of the fragment's audio/video PTS starts and retains separate DTS. This is useful for a common multiplexed segment timeline.
- hls.js `assignProgramDateTime()` propagates a known fragment's programDateTime forward to the next fragment by adding the previous fragment's duration. It does not inherently backfill fragments preceding the first tagged fragment.
- hls.js `mergeDetails()` preserves PTS/DTS metadata for overlapping fragments and has a program-date-time merge path for certain delta playlist updates, but the app must not assume every older fragment will always have a non-null programDateTime after a cold start or discontinuity.

### Required HLS timestamp-map implementation behaviour

1. On every playlist refresh, inspect all current fragments and parts. As soon as a fragment has a valid explicit or propagated programDateTime, store its mapping by camera path, discontinuity counter (`cc`), media sequence (`sn`), fragment URI and start/end PTS.
2. Keep the application's own rolling map for at least the full 30-second bookmark/replay horizon plus the current live-delay window. Do not rely on the current playlist retaining PDT tags or old fragments.
3. For older untagged fragments at cold start, infer programDateTime backwards from a later known tagged fragment using the intervening EXTINF durations only if there is no discontinuity, gap, skipped segment or unaccounted timeline reset. Mark inferred mappings as lower confidence until validated.
4. If there is an `EXT-X-DISCONTINUITY`, sequence reset, gap, or unexplained PTS drift, start a new mapping epoch and do not propagate timestamps across the boundary without independent evidence.
5. For the exact displayed frame, locate the containing fragment using `requestVideoFrameCallback().mediaTime` and the hls.js video/combined PTS range. Candidate mapping is `PDT + (mediaTime - fragment.startPTS) * 1000`; verify that the fragment PDT anchor and hls.js `startPTS` refer to the same first presentation sample. No-B-frame input simplifies this because PTS and DTS coincide; audio/video start offsets still need measurement.
6. Compare the HLS-derived NTP with MediaMTX fMP4 segment NTP and the RTSP sidecar's `PacketNTP()` mapping. Store the mapping confidence/uncertainty with the bookmark.

### Assessment

HLS can provide a usable live-frame-to-NTP mapping, but low-latency playlists do not carry explicit PDT on every segment. A persistent application-side map is required; cold-start backfill must be guarded against discontinuities. This is a manageable implementation detail, not a reason to reject HLS, but the mapping must be tested before it is used for referee bookmarks. No architecture is selected. Overall status remains **CONDITIONAL GO for isolated PoCs only; NO-GO for architecture lock/full implementation**.

### Evidence

- gohlslib v2.4.7 low-latency playlist PDT serialization: https://github.com/bluenviron/gohlslib/blob/v2.4.7/muxer_stream.go
- hls.js Fragment metadata and PTS/DTS fields: https://github.com/video-dev/hls.js/blob/127c6adee512843abf85748d82767bdf1a3c8e5c/src/loader/fragment.ts
- hls.js PTS/DTS aggregation and fragment timing: https://github.com/video-dev/hls.js/blob/127c6adee512843abf85748d82767bdf1a3c8e5c/src/utils/level-helper.ts
- hls.js programDateTime propagation: https://github.com/video-dev/hls.js/blob/127c6adee512843abf85748d82767bdf1a3c8e5c/src/loader/m3u8-parser.ts
## 2026-11-15 — MediaMTX LL-HLS default PART-HOLD-BACK and 1–5 second hls.js target feasibility

**Scope:** Exact source inspection of MediaMTX v1.21.3 defaults, gohlslib v2.4.7 playlist generation and hls.js live-latency configuration. No runtime PoC or local changes.

### Verified defaults and formula

- MediaMTX v1.21.3 default HLS variant is `lowLatency`, `hlsSegmentCount` is 7, `hlsSegmentDuration` is 1 second and `hlsPartDuration` is 200 milliseconds.
- gohlslib v2.4.7 calculates LL-HLS `PART-HOLD-BACK` as `2.5 * partTargetDuration`. With the default 200 ms part target, the advertised part holdback is **500 ms**.
- hls.js supports an explicit `liveSyncDuration` target in seconds. When explicitly set, it takes precedence over the manifest-derived holdback/segment-count target. hls.js documentation warns that a target below roughly three segment/part target durations may cause stalls; for the default 200 ms part target, three parts are 600 ms, so a 1-second target is not below that rough part-based floor.
- The 7-segment default playlist retains more than the 5-second maximum Live Delay target at the default 1-second segment duration, but actual available duration and part retention must be checked in the generated playlist and under stalls/discontinuities.

### Implications

- HLS/hls.js is a credible single live-view candidate for all 1–5 second Live Delay selections. Unlike `RTCRtpReceiver.jitterBufferTarget`, `liveSyncDuration` has no 4,000 ms API setter cap.
- The default 500 ms PART-HOLD-BACK establishes the low-latency live edge; an explicit 1–5 second hls.js target can hold playback farther behind that edge. This is still a player target, not a guaranteed actual end-to-end delay.
- For 1 second, the target is above 3 x 200 ms part target, but actual stability depends on part availability, playlist reload cadence, network jitter, decoder throughput and hls.js recovery policy. Do not infer success from the formula alone.
- For 5 seconds, 7 x 1-second segments provide a nominal playlist window, but verify that the current playlist actually contains the requested position and that hls.js does not seek forward to liveSyncPosition.
- If hls.js uses playback-rate catch-up or a seek-to-live recovery, the visible stream may repeat/drop frames or jump. Capture the actual displayed frame timestamp and record these events; do not silently preserve the requested target while showing a different media time.

### Required PoC

Test `liveSyncDuration` values 1, 2, 3, 4 and 5 seconds with MediaMTX v1.21.3 default LL-HLS settings and the actual two-camera streams. Record actual `requestVideoFrameCallback` frame NTP, live edge NTP, `video.currentTime`, hls.js `liveSyncPosition`, playbackRate, buffer ranges, part/fragment load times, stalls, dropped/repeated frames, CPU/GPU and memory. Repeat with network jitter/loss and MediaMTX restart. Verify the bookmark maps to the actual displayed frame and to the fMP4 archive.

### Assessment

LL-HLS/hls.js is now a strong candidate for the visible live view and all five Live Delay options, with the separate WebRTC replay worker preserving instant replay and exact H.264 access-unit history. It avoids the 4-second `jitterBufferTarget` cap, and MediaMTX already emits ProgramDateTime for archive mapping. Its target latency and exact frame-to-PDT mapping still need runtime validation. No architecture is selected. Overall status remains **CONDITIONAL GO for isolated PoCs only; NO-GO for architecture lock/full implementation**.

### Evidence

- MediaMTX v1.21.3 HLS defaults: https://github.com/bluenviron/mediamtx/blob/v1.21.3/internal/conf/conf.go
- gohlslib v2.4.7 PART-HOLD-BACK calculation: https://github.com/bluenviron/gohlslib/blob/v2.4.7/muxer_stream.go
- hls.js live latency API: https://github.com/video-dev/hls.js/blob/master/docs/API.md
## 2026-11-15 — Alternative replay PoC: independent HLS/MSE player over an extended MediaMTX segment window

**Scope:** Feasibility analysis based on MediaMTX v1.21.3 HLS playlist retention, gohlslib low-latency segments, and hls.js independent player/start-position APIs. Alternative only; no runtime PoC or architecture decision.

### Candidate topology

- Keep the visible live HLS player independent.
- Increase MediaMTX `hlsSegmentCount` from the default 7 to a value that retains the required 5–30-second history plus live-delay margin (the exact count must be based on actual segment durations, because segments rotate on random-access points and can exceed the nominal 1-second target).
- Create a second hls.js instance and `<video>` element for replay. It uses the same MediaMTX HLS path/playlist but an independent MediaSource and playback timeline. The replay player can start at a media time derived from the bookmark's NTP/PDT mapping and can be paused/seeked without changing the live player.
- The HLS playlist/segments are the compressed history store; no additional WebRTC replay reader, encoded transform or custom H.264 ring is required for this variant.
- Use `requestVideoFrameCallback()` on the replay video to record actual output `mediaTime` values. Build a compact per-camera frame-PTS index while the player is warm. When stepping, seek to the next/previous known PTS and verify the actual output PTS returned by the next callback.

### Potential advantages

- Uses MediaMTX's existing HLS muxer and compressed fMP4 segments; no custom encoded-frame copy/ring is needed.
- Live and replay use independent HTML video elements/MediaSource instances, so replay pause/seek does not alter the live player's position.
- MediaMTX HLS ProgramDateTime and hls.js fragment PTS metadata can map the selected frame to the same server NTP timeline used by the archive.
- Browser-native MSE/H.264 decoding may use platform hardware acceleration without a separate WebCodecs decoder configuration path.
- A 30–40-second compressed HLS window is likely much smaller than a full decoded-frame cache. At an illustrative 8 Mbit/s, 30 seconds is about 30 MB per camera before container/metadata overhead; actual bitrate and segment sizes must be measured.

### Risks and unresolved requirements

- MediaMTX's default 7-segment HLS playlist is too short for 15–30-second replay. The configured segment count must be increased, and actual retained duration must be verified across long GOPs, reconnects and discontinuities.
- The HLS player may not expose every decoded frame through `requestVideoFrameCallback()` if the decoder/compositor drops frames under load. A timestamp index built only from displayed callbacks may omit encoded frames needed for exact frame-step.
- Setting `video.currentTime` to a previously recorded PTS may seek from a preceding keyframe and decode forward. It may be fast, but exact adjacent-frame stepping in both directions is not guaranteed by the HTML media API and must be measured.
- The browser's MSE decoder does not provide a direct public `stepFrame()` method. `currentTime` seeking plus callback verification is an experimental approach; if the actual output PTS differs from the target, repeated correction may add latency.
- A replay player that is paused for a long review may become far behind the moving live edge. It must preserve the paused buffer while the match continues, then reset/re-warm to the configured replay lookback on close. HLS.js latency catch-up and MSE buffer eviction must not silently jump the visible replay frame.
- Two HLS players per camera (live plus replay) still consume decoder/GPU resources. With two cameras, measure four simultaneous video pipelines and the effect on live/recording.
- Low-Latency HLS playlist PDT tags are only present on the latest two segments in the MediaMTX default variant; the app must maintain its own rolling PDT/PTS map and must not rely on the current playlist alone.

### Required PoC comparison

Compare this HLS/MSE replay variant against the direct WebRTC encoded ring + WebCodecs decoder:

1. Two live HLS players plus two independent replay HLS players; set `hlsSegmentCount` to retain at least 30 seconds plus margin.
2. Replay requests at random offsets (5/15/30 seconds) while the live match continues. Measure button-to-first-displayed-frame and verify that live position never changes.
3. Build a frame PTS index during warm playback, then test exact next/previous frame stepping and repeated rapid stepping. Compare every output `mediaTime` against the requested PTS.
4. Pause replay for 5, 15 and 30 seconds while the source continues. Verify the paused frame remains stable and the replay can recover to the configured lookback without a visible jump.
5. Stress both cameras, target resolution/fps, HLS playlist reloads, segment eviction, reconnects, B-frame/no-B-frame sources, MSE quota errors and browser hardware decode.
6. Compare CPU/GPU, memory, MSE buffer ranges, decoded frames, dropped/repeated frames, first-frame latency, exact frame-step and live/recording continuity.

### Assessment

An independent HLS/MSE replay player is a credible lower-complexity fallback because MediaMTX already stores a rolling compressed segment window and HLS playback is separate from the live player. It may meet immediate replay with a warm player and short GOPs, but exact frame-step and rapid seek behaviour are not guaranteed by the browser media API. The direct WebRTC encoded ring + WebCodecs path remains the stronger candidate for exact frame control; HLS/MSE should be tested as a simpler comparator before committing to custom decoding. No architecture is selected. Overall status remains **CONDITIONAL GO for isolated PoCs only; NO-GO for architecture lock/full implementation**.

### Evidence

- MediaMTX v1.21.3 HLS defaults and segment-count configuration: https://github.com/bluenviron/mediamtx/blob/v1.21.3/internal/conf/conf.go
- MediaMTX v1.21.3 HLS muxer path: https://github.com/bluenviron/mediamtx/blob/v1.21.3/internal/protocols/hls/from_stream.go
- gohlslib v2.4.7 low-latency playlist/segment window: https://github.com/bluenviron/gohlslib/blob/v2.4.7/muxer_stream.go
- hls.js start-position and latency control: https://github.com/video-dev/hls.js/blob/127c6adee512843abf85748d82767bdf1a3c8e5c/src/controller/base-stream-controller.ts
- hls.js live latency controller behaviour: https://github.com/video-dev/hls.js/blob/127c6adee512843abf85748d82767bdf1a3c8e5c/src/controller/latency-controller.ts
## 2026-11-15 — Candidate replay ingest: MediaMTX LL-HLS fMP4 parts → MP4Box.js → WebCodecs

**Scope:** Feasibility review of MediaMTX v1.21.3 HLS fMP4 output, gohlslib LL-HLS parts, current MP4Box.js sample-extraction API, and W3C WebCodecs AVC registration. Source-level candidate only; no real camera fragment was parsed and no runtime PoC/local changes were made.

### Core idea

Use the existing MediaMTX Low-Latency HLS output as the replay worker's encoded source. A dedicated worker fetches the HLS init segment and successive LL-HLS parts, parses their fragmented MP4 samples with MP4Box.js, stores H.264 access units in a bounded compressed ring, and decodes selected sequences with WebCodecs. The visible live HLS/hls.js player remains independent and continues playing while the replay worker seeks/pauses/steps.

### Verified technical building blocks

- MediaMTX v1.21.3 HLS output passes the stream unit's `u.NTP` and `u.PTS` into gohlslib. gohlslib v2.4.7 produces fragmented MP4 LL-HLS segments/parts and advertises `EXT-X-PROGRAM-DATE-TIME` from segment `startNTP`.
- MP4Box.js supports progressive parsing and fragmented MP4. Its API supports `createFile()`, `appendBuffer()` with a monotonically increasing `fileStart`, `onReady()`, `setExtractionOptions()`, `start()` and `onSamples()`.
- MP4Box.js sample extraction exposes per-sample `timescale`, `dts`, `cts`, `duration`, `is_rap` and sample `data`. `cts` is presentation time; `dts` is decode time. The samples are extracted in MP4 track sample/decode order.
- MP4Box.js documents `createFile(true)` as retaining all mdat data when needed. For a streaming HLS init segment followed by media parts, a PoC should initialize from the `EXT-X-MAP` init segment, call `onReady()`, configure extraction and `start()` before appending media parts; this may allow the default non-retaining mode and prompt sample release. Verify with actual MediaMTX parts.
- The current MP4Box.js repository is under BSD-3-Clause. This is a permissive license, but the exact pinned version and transitive runtime dependency/license inventory still need review.
- The W3C AVC WebCodecs registration defines `avc`-format chunks as canonical length-prefixed H.264 access units and uses an `AVCDecoderConfigurationRecord` (`avcC`) as `VideoDecoderConfig.description`. SPS/PPS are carried in the description rather than repeated in every sample.
- The AVC registration expects a `key` chunk in `avc` format to contain an IDR picture. MP4Box.js `is_rap` must therefore be checked against actual H.264 NAL types/IDR status before mapping it to `EncodedVideoChunk.type = 'key'`.
- `EncodedVideoChunk.timestamp` is a presentation timestamp in microseconds. The H.264 MP4 sample's `cts` should be converted using `sample.cts * 1_000_000 / sample.timescale`; its `dts` remains useful for verifying decode order and discontinuities. Input chunks must be submitted in decode order, with PTS timestamps; WebCodecs outputs decoded frames in presentation order.
- This path uses MP4/AVC (`avcC` + length-prefixed sample data), not Annex-B RTP access units. It can therefore avoid the MediaMTX v1.21.3 WebRTC H.264 B-frame rejection, provided the HLS/fMP4 sample order and PTS/DTS are preserved and the browser's WebCodecs H.264 implementation supports the actual profile/level.

### Candidate pipeline

1. Live view: hls.js plays MediaMTX LL-HLS independently, with a measured `liveSyncDuration` target of 1–5 seconds. `requestVideoFrameCallback().mediaTime` identifies the frame actually shown.
2. Replay ingest: a DedicatedWorker fetches the HLS playlist, init segment (`EXT-X-MAP`) and each new `EXT-X-PART`/completed segment using the same camera path. Prefer LL-HLS blocking reload where supported rather than aggressive polling.
3. Demux: initialize MP4Box.js from the init segment; after `onReady`, select the video track, extract samples and start parsing before appending media parts. Track each part/segment by discontinuity sequence, media sequence, part index and URI to avoid duplicate samples when a completed segment repeats earlier parts.
4. Sample record: retain camera/source epoch, codec-config epoch, sample decode order, `dts`, `cts`, `duration`, timescale, random-access flag, byte length, source segment/part identity and the fragment's PDT/NTP mapping.
5. Compressed ring: retain at least the configured 5–30-second replay horizon, bounded by both time and bytes. Evict only at a valid random-access boundary so every retained delta sample has a preceding usable IDR and matching `avcC`/SPS/PPS configuration.
6. Decoder: configure WebCodecs `VideoDecoder` using MP4Box.js track codec string and `avcC` description. Submit samples in decode order with `timestamp` derived from CTS and `duration` from sample duration. Bound `decodeQueueSize`; monitor errors, saturation and output cadence.
7. Display/frame-step: index decoded `VideoFrame`s by their PTS in presentation order. Keep a short bounded decoded hot window; for an older requested position, start from the preceding IDR in the compressed ring and decode forward. Exact adjacent stepping is by decoded-frame index, never by adding a fixed 1/30-second increment.
8. Bookmark: map the live HLS `mediaTime` through hls.js fragment `startPTS` and `programDateTime` to the same PTS/NTP timeline, then find the corresponding sample in the worker ring. For bookmarks created in replay, use the selected decoded frame's PTS/NTP.
9. Archive: compare HLS PDT/NTP and sample PTS/DTS against MediaMTX's continuous fMP4 recorder, which receives the same `u.NTP` and stream timestamps. Preserve PTS/DTS offsets for B-frame samples; do not assume PTS equals DTS.

### Potential advantages versus WebRTC encoded transform

- Avoids `RTCRtpScriptTransform` in the WebRTC receive path and its low-latency/no-backpressure constraints.
- Avoids opening a second WebRTC PeerConnection per camera for replay and avoids MediaMTX's per-reader WebRTC GOP/keyframe-join limitations.
- Avoids MediaMTX v1.21.3's specific WebRTC H.264 B-frame rejection; HLS/fMP4 plus WebCodecs can represent decode order and presentation timestamps separately.
- Reuses MediaMTX's existing LL-HLS output and the same fMP4/PTS/NTP timeline as the archive.
- Uses a pure-JavaScript MP4 parser plus WebCodecs; no FFmpeg binary is inherently required for this replay pipeline.
- The worker's HTTP requests read the existing HLS muxer output; they do not create a separate MediaMTX `stream.Reader` per replay operation. They add local HTTP/loopback traffic and parser/decode CPU, which still must be measured.
- The live HLS player and replay WebCodecs worker have independent state. Pausing, seeking or stepping in the replay worker cannot move the live player's playhead.

### Risks and hard gates

- This has not yet been proven against an actual MediaMTX v1.21.3 LL-HLS part. Confirm the init segment, `moof`/`mdat` structure, track ID/timescale, `avcC`, sample extraction and `tfdt`/`trun` timestamps with the real camera stream.
- Confirm MP4Box.js handles MediaMTX's repeated partial fragments, incremental `fileStart` offsets, playlist reloads and discontinuities without retaining unbounded mdat/sample buffers. Release extracted samples promptly and bound every queue.
- The HLS parts are only available after part closure (default part target 200 ms) plus HTTP delivery. The replay worker must remain warm; this is not a per-click fetch/decode design.
- The WebCodecs AVC registration is non-normative and browsers are not required to support H.264. Use `VideoDecoder.isConfigSupported()` and actual decode tests for the exact codec string/profile/level, Windows/Linux browser and GPU.
- For B-frames, samples must be fed in decode order with PTS timestamps. Validate non-monotonic CTS in decode order, decoder output presentation order, and correct exact stepping both directions. If MP4Box.js extraction order is not the required decode order for the actual parts, reject the path.
- Validate `is_rap` against actual IDR NAL units. A recovery-point SEI or non-IDR sample must not be mislabeled as an AVC key chunk.
- MediaMTX's upstream single stored SPS/PPS pair/multiple-PPS risk remains because HLS uses the same MediaMTX stream-format/remux path. Test multiple PPS IDs, parameter-set changes and reconnects.
- MP4Box.js parser input is media data from the camera path. Run it in a bounded worker, enforce box/sample size limits, handle malformed fragments and reset on codec/track discontinuity.
- The live HLS player and replay WebCodecs decoder still compete for CPU/GPU and decoder resources. Measure two cameras and simultaneous replay; no impact on live/recording is proven.
- HLS PDT tags and fragment PTS mapping must remain valid across LL-HLS part updates, playlist delta/reload, discontinuity, PTS offset and segment rotation. The app-side PDT map described above remains required.
- Cross-camera sync remains limited by independent RTMP NTP-estimator anchors and source-to-server latency; this pipeline improves per-camera archive mapping but does not prove true sensor synchronization.

### Required PoC order

1. Capture one real MediaMTX v1.21.3 HLS init segment and consecutive LL-HLS parts from a real camera. Parse with MP4Box.js in a worker. Log `avcC`, codec string, timescale, sample count, DTS, CTS, duration, `is_rap`, sample byte length and discontinuities.
2. Feed the extracted samples into WebCodecs. First test no-B-frame source, then B-frame source if available. Verify IDR startup, H.264 profile support, decoded frame count, presentation order, PTS correctness and decoder error recovery.
3. Build a 30-second compressed sample ring with safe RAP-boundary eviction. Verify bounded memory, no gaps/duplicates and recovery after missing/corrupt parts and camera reconnect.
4. Decode on demand from preceding IDR to random target PTS. Measure replay button-to-first-display, forward/backward exact frame step, rapid repeated stepping, slow motion and decoder reset/reuse.
5. Compare live HLS `requestVideoFrameCallback().mediaTime`/PDT mapping with worker sample PTS and later with the fMP4 archive. Verify exact bookmark frame identity.
6. Run two cameras at target resolution/fps/bitrate, with one and two simultaneous replay operations. Measure CPU, GPU, memory, HLS part lag, decode queue, first-frame latency, frame drops, live delay and recording continuity.
7. Compare against the separate WebRTC reader + encoded transform + WebCodecs approach. Select only on measured end-to-end performance and compatibility.

### Assessment

This is currently the strongest new architecture candidate to test: LL-HLS provides a compressed, timestamped source already used by MediaMTX; MP4Box.js extracts individual access units; WebCodecs provides exact decoded frame order and step control. It could remove the need for a second WebRTC reader and bypass MediaMTX v1.21.3's WebRTC B-frame limitation while preserving independent live/replay state. However, real-part parsing, sample order, H.264 parameter sets, browser codec support, memory bounds and first-frame latency are unproven. It is a PoC candidate, not an approved architecture. Overall status remains **CONDITIONAL GO for isolated PoCs only; NO-GO for architecture lock/full implementation**.

### Evidence

- MediaMTX v1.21.3 HLS output passes NTP/PTS: https://github.com/bluenviron/mediamtx/blob/v1.21.3/internal/protocols/hls/from_stream.go
- gohlslib v2.4.7 LL-HLS/fMP4 generation: https://github.com/bluenviron/gohlslib/blob/v2.4.7/muxer_stream.go
- MP4Box.js API: progressive parsing, fragmented MP4 and sample extraction: https://github.com/gpac/mp4box.js/
- MP4Box.js BSD-3-Clause license: https://github.com/gpac/mp4box.js/blob/main/LICENSE
- W3C AVC WebCodecs registration: AVCC samples, avcC description and IDR key chunks: https://www.w3.org/TR/webcodecs-avc-codec-registration/
- W3C WebCodecs VideoDecoder output order, PTS and resource handling: https://www.w3.org/TR/webcodecs/
## 2026-11-15 — LL-HLS segment PDT is anchored to the leading video track's random-access sample

**Scope:** Exact source inspection of gohlslib v2.4.7 `muxer.go` and `muxer_segmenter.go`. This clarifies the HLS/PDT mapping proposed for the MP4Box.js/WebCodecs replay path; source-level only, no runtime PoC.

### Verified source behaviour

- gohlslib v2.4.7 chooses the first video track as the HLS muxer's leading track when a video track exists. Audio is not the leading track in a normal audio/video camera stream.
- The H.264 fMP4 segmenter identifies random-access access units using `h264.IsRandomAccess()`, derives DTS using `h264.DTSExtractor`, and writes each MP4 sample with `PTSOffset = PTS - DTS`, `dts`, and the supplied NTP time.
- The segmenter holds one sample in `fmp4NextSample` to calculate sample duration. When a new random-access video sample arrives after the minimum segment duration, it rotates the segment using that leading video sample's DTS and NTP. The IDR/random-access sample becomes the start of the new segment.
- `muxer_stream` stores the segment's `startDTS` and `startNTP`; the LL-HLS playlist serializes `startNTP` as `EXT-X-PROGRAM-DATE-TIME`.
- Therefore, for a normal H.264 video-leading LL-HLS stream, a segment's PDT is anchored to the NTP of its leading video random-access sample, not to an arbitrary first audio sample. The first video sample's PTS is recoverable from MP4Box.js sample `cts`; its DTS and PTSOffset are also available.

### Consequence for HLS bookmark mapping

- For each HLS segment, identify the first video random-access sample in decode order and retain its `cts`, `dts`, `timescale`, and the segment's `programDateTime`.
- Candidate sample-to-NTP mapping is `NTP(sample) = segmentPDT + (sample.cts - firstRAP.cts) / timescale`. This uses presentation time, not DTS, and preserves B-frame composition offsets.
- The live hls.js player's video `requestVideoFrameCallback().mediaTime` must be mapped to the same segment/video PTS range (prefer the video-track PTS range from hls.js parsing metadata, not a combined audio/video start time). Match the resulting PTS to MP4Box.js samples in the replay ring.
- Verify the mapping across B-frame streams, audio/video offsets, segment rotation, LL-HLS parts, and the fMP4 archive. Do not assume `frag.startPTS` always equals the first RAP PTS if B-frame presentation reordering or track offsets are present.

### Assessment

This removes one uncertainty in the HLS timestamp design: MediaMTX/gohlslib deliberately uses the leading video track and starts each fMP4 segment at a video random-access sample, so the playlist PDT is a useful video-frame NTP anchor. The exact PTS mapping remains to be verified with real fMP4 parts and hls.js callback timestamps. No architecture is selected. Overall status remains **CONDITIONAL GO for isolated PoCs only; NO-GO for architecture lock/full implementation**.

### Evidence

- gohlslib v2.4.7 leading-track selection (video preferred): https://github.com/bluenviron/gohlslib/blob/v2.4.7/muxer.go
- gohlslib v2.4.7 H.264 DTS/PTSOffset and random-access segment rotation: https://github.com/bluenviron/gohlslib/blob/v2.4.7/muxer_segmenter.go
- gohlslib v2.4.7 PDT playlist serialization: https://github.com/bluenviron/gohlslib/blob/v2.4.7/muxer_stream.go
## 2026-11-15 — H.264 recovery-point SEI is not equivalent to a WebCodecs AVC IDR key chunk

**Scope:** Exact source inspection of MediaMTX v1.21.3/gohlslib H.264 random-access handling and the current W3C AVC WebCodecs registration. Source-level compatibility finding; no runtime PoC.

### Verified findings

- MediaMTX's H.264 `IsRandomAccess()` implementation returns true for an IDR NAL unit. It also returns true when an SEI recovery-point message appears before a subsequent non-IDR slice in the access unit.
