- gohlslib v2.4.7 uses `h264.IsRandomAccess()` when marking H.264 fMP4 samples as sync/non-sync and when deciding whether to rotate an HLS segment. Therefore an HLS segment can begin at a recovery-point access unit that is not an IDR picture.
- MP4Box.js exposes a sample-level `is_rap` flag derived from MP4 sync-sample metadata. That flag may therefore describe a MediaMTX recovery-point sample, not necessarily an IDR.
- The W3C AVC WebCodecs registration states that an `EncodedVideoChunk` marked `type: 'key'` in `avc` format is expected to contain an IDR picture. It does not define recovery-point SEI as a substitute for an IDR key chunk.

### Consequences for HLS/MP4Box/WebCodecs replay

1. Do not map MP4Box.js `sample.is_rap` or the MP4 sync-sample flag directly to `EncodedVideoChunk.type = 'key'` without inspecting the H.264 NAL units.
2. Parse the length-prefixed NAL units in each sample. Distinguish at least IDR NAL type 5, non-IDR slices, and recovery-point SEI messages.
3. For a WebCodecs decoder reset/start, use only a verified IDR access unit as the `key` chunk, with the matching `avcC`/SPS/PPS configuration.
4. A recovery-point access unit may be retained as a `delta` chunk when decoding continues from an earlier valid IDR and the full dependency chain is present. Do not treat it as a standalone decoder restart point.
5. Ring eviction must retain the preceding actual IDR and all dependent access units. A MediaMTX/HLS segment boundary marked by recovery-point SEI is not necessarily a safe WebCodecs ring eviction boundary.
6. If the actual PRISM source emits recovery points but no sufficiently frequent IDRs, measure time-to-next-IDR and determine whether this makes on-demand replay too slow. This is a source compatibility gate.

### Required PoC tests

- Inspect every MP4Box-extracted H.264 sample's NAL types and compare actual IDR presence with MP4 `is_rap`/sync flags.
- Feed an actual recovery-point sample to WebCodecs as `delta` after a prior IDR and verify decoding; separately confirm that marking the same sample as `key` is rejected or fails to produce valid output.
- Reset/recreate the decoder at actual IDR only and verify correct first decoded frame.
- Evict the compressed ring at both MP4 sync-sample boundaries and actual IDR boundaries; verify that only the latter guarantees a self-contained WebCodecs decode start.
- Test actual PRISM streams for IDR interval, recovery-point interval, SPS/PPS availability and reconnect behaviour.

### Assessment

This does not invalidate the HLS/fMP4 → MP4Box.js → WebCodecs candidate, but it tightens its keyframe and ring-eviction logic. MediaMTX's broader random-access classification is suitable for its own HLS/MSE pipeline but cannot be assumed to satisfy WebCodecs AVC's stricter IDR key-chunk contract. No architecture is selected. Overall status remains **CONDITIONAL GO for isolated PoCs only; NO-GO for architecture lock/full implementation**.

### Evidence

- MediaMTX v1.21.3 H.264 random-access handling: https://github.com/bluenviron/mediamtx/blob/v1.21.3/internal/protocols/hls/from_stream.go
- mediacommon H.264 `IsRandomAccess()` includes recovery-point SEI: https://github.com/bluenviron/mediacommon/blob/059ab2e28705f0c8cac17465bf969ee39d9ba73d/pkg/codecs/h264/is_random_access.go
- gohlslib v2.4.7 H.264 random-access and sync-sample logic: https://github.com/bluenviron/gohlslib/blob/v2.4.7/muxer_segmenter.go
- W3C AVC WebCodecs registration, IDR requirement for `type: 'key'`: https://www.w3.org/TR/webcodecs-avc-codec-registration/
## 2026-11-15 — HLS fMP4 codec configuration is initialized once; midstream SPS/PPS changes require explicit reinitialization proof

**Scope:** Exact source inspection of MediaMTX v1.21.3 HLS muxer instance creation and gohlslib v2.4.7 H.264 sample-entry initialization. Source-level risk; no runtime PoC.

### Verified source behaviour

- MediaMTX v1.21.3 creates an HLS `muxerInstance` with a `stream.Reader`, calls `hls.FromStream()` using the stream's current original/output descriptions, constructs gohlslib tracks from those descriptions, starts the muxer and registers the reader. The HLS muxer instance owns one gohlslib muxer and one reader for the path.
- gohlslib v2.4.7 H.264 track initialization collects SPS/PPS from incoming access units only while the initialization file has not yet been written. Once the init file is present, its H.264 sample entry/`avcC` is not automatically rebuilt from a later SPS/PPS change by that code path.
- MediaMTX's H.264 stream remuxer separately stores one current SPS and one current PPS and can prepend them at random-access points, but this does not by itself prove that an already-written HLS init segment's `avcC` is updated or that a new `EXT-X-MAP` is emitted.
- The inspected HLS muxer instance setup is based on a snapshot of the stream descriptions at creation. The reviewed code does not establish that an in-place SPS/PPS/profile/resolution change during an active HLS muxer automatically destroys/recreates the muxer or emits a new initialization segment.

### Consequences for HLS/MP4Box/WebCodecs

- A new SPS/PPS or profile/level/resolution configuration must be treated as a codec-configuration epoch change. The replay worker must not continue decoding new samples with an old `avcC` description.
- Before accepting the HLS/MP4Box/WebCodecs path, verify whether MediaMTX v1.21.3 responds to midstream SPS/PPS changes by restarting the HLS muxer, emitting a new `EXT-X-MAP` and discontinuity, or continuing with stale initialization metadata.
- If a new init segment is emitted, the worker must reset MP4Box parsing state as needed, create a new decoder configuration epoch and resume only from a valid IDR with matching SPS/PPS.
- If no new init segment is emitted, the app must reject the changed stream/mark the camera unavailable for replay rather than silently feeding mismatched samples to WebCodecs.
- Camera reconnect is a separate case: a new HLS muxer/init segment is expected and must start a new source/codec epoch.

### Required PoC tests

1. Start a camera stream and capture the initial HLS init segment and `avcC`.
2. Change resolution/profile/SPS/PPS without disconnecting the RTMP publisher if the source permits it; observe MediaMTX HLS muxer lifecycle, playlist discontinuity, `EXT-X-MAP` URI and new init bytes.
3. Change PPS ID while retaining the same resolution and profile; inspect whether the new SPS/PPS is present in the HLS init segment and every IDR sample.
4. Reconnect the camera and verify that a new muxer/init segment and codec epoch are visible to the replay worker.
5. Confirm MP4Box.js and WebCodecs are reconfigured only at explicit codec epochs and that bookmarks do not cross the epoch boundary without a new mapping.

### Assessment

The HLS/fMP4 → MP4Box.js → WebCodecs candidate still looks promising, but it has a new hard gate: midstream codec-configuration changes must produce a matching new init segment or a controlled muxer restart. The current source review does not prove this behaviour. This risk joins the existing multiple-PPS and IDR/recovery-point tests. No architecture is selected. Overall status remains **CONDITIONAL GO for isolated PoCs only; NO-GO for architecture lock/full implementation**.

### Evidence

- MediaMTX v1.21.3 HLS muxer instance and one-time stream-description setup: https://github.com/bluenviron/mediamtx/blob/v1.21.3/internal/servers/hls/muxer_instance.go
- MediaMTX v1.21.3 HLS track creation from stream descriptions: https://github.com/bluenviron/mediamtx/blob/v1.21.3/internal/protocols/hls/from_stream.go
- gohlslib v2.4.7 H.264 SPS/PPS handling and init-file guard: https://github.com/bluenviron/gohlslib/blob/v2.4.7/muxer_segmenter.go
- MediaMTX v1.21.3 stored H.264 parameter-set behaviour: https://github.com/bluenviron/mediamtx/blob/v1.21.3/internal/stream/format_updater.go
## 2026-11-15 — MP4Box.js packaging and licensing fit for the HLS/WebCodecs PoC

**Scope:** Review of the pinned MP4Box.js v2.5.0 package metadata. No package installed, built or executed; no local files changed.

### Verified package facts

- MP4Box.js v2.5.0 declares `BSD-3-Clause` and publishes prebuilt JavaScript distributions (`dist/mp4box.all.*` and `dist/mp4box.simple.*`).
- Its package metadata lists no runtime `dependencies`; the listed dependencies are development/build/test tooling. The package declares a Node.js engine requirement for its package tooling, but the browser distribution is intended to be loaded by web applications. The PoC should consume a pinned prebuilt browser bundle and must not require Node at runtime.
- The `simple` export is a potential smaller parsing-only entry point, but its exact feature set/size must be checked before selecting it. Otherwise use the standard prebuilt distribution.
- The HLS/WebCodecs replay pipeline does not inherently require an FFmpeg executable: MP4Box.js parses fMP4 in the browser worker and WebCodecs performs H.264 decoding. This only removes FFmpeg from this particular replay path; it does not prove FFmpeg is unnecessary for every other possible media function.

### Consequences

- Pin MP4Box.js to a released version and bundle the exact prebuilt JS file locally for offline operation. Do not load from a CDN or resolve an npm package at runtime.
- Include the BSD-3-Clause license and preserve required copyright/notice text in the distribution.
- Audit the selected build artifact and all bundled runtime code; do not assume package-level devDependencies are shipped or that the package metadata alone completes the project's third-party license inventory.
- Compare bundle size and extraction performance for `mp4box.simple` versus the full distribution before choosing.

### Evidence

- MP4Box.js v2.5.0 package metadata and BSD-3-Clause declaration: https://github.com/gpac/mp4box.js/blob/v2.5.0/package.json
- MP4Box.js v2.5.0 license: https://github.com/gpac/mp4box.js/blob/v2.5.0/LICENSE
- MP4Box.js browser/progressive parsing and extraction documentation: https://github.com/gpac/mp4box.js/tree/v2.5.0
## 2026-11-15 — gohlslib LL-HLS parts are complete fMP4 fragments, but INDEPENDENT is not an IDR guarantee

**Scope:** Exact source inspection of gohlslib v2.4.7 `muxer_part.go` and `muxer_segment_fmp4.go`. Source-level validation of the MP4Box.js input format; no runtime parse test.

### Verified part structure

- When an LL-HLS part is finalized, gohlslib creates an `fmp4.Part` containing a sequence number and per-track `PartTrack` entries with track ID, base decode time and samples, then serializes it with `Part.Marshal()` to the part storage.
- The part is therefore a complete fragmented-MP4 media fragment (moof/mdat payload) that can be appended after the playlist's `EXT-X-MAP` initialization segment. This is compatible in principle with MP4Box.js incremental fragmented-MP4 parsing.
- The part storage is separate from the init segment and the HLS segment playlist entry; the worker must retain the correct init-segment/codec epoch and append each part exactly once.
- gohlslib sets `EXT-X-PART:INDEPENDENT=YES` when the leading track (video when present) has a sample whose MP4 `IsNonSyncSample` flag is false. Since MediaMTX can classify recovery-point SEI as random access and mark the resulting sample sync, `INDEPENDENT=YES` is not proof that the part starts with a WebCodecs-compatible IDR.

### Consequences

- The HLS worker can feed each finalized `EXT-X-PART` payload to MP4Box.js as a media fragment after the matching `EXT-X-MAP` init segment; it does not need to wait for the full HLS segment to close.
- Maintain a part identity `(camera, discontinuity sequence, media sequence, part index, URI)` and reject duplicates. If the worker starts mid-segment, fetch the required earlier part(s) or wait for a verified IDR before decoding.
- Do not use `EXT-X-PART:INDEPENDENT=YES`, MP4Box `is_rap`, or the MP4 sync flag alone as the decoder-reset/keyframe decision. Inspect the H.264 NAL types and require an actual IDR for a fresh WebCodecs AVC decoder.
- The part's `BaseTime`/sample DTS and sample CTS are the timing inputs for WebCodecs; preserve both and feed in decode order.

### Required PoC

- Capture one real `EXT-X-MAP` plus consecutive `EXT-X-PART` responses from MediaMTX v1.21.3 and parse them incrementally with MP4Box.js. Verify the parser emits the same video samples and DTS/CTS as parsing the completed segment.
- Compare HLS `INDEPENDENT` and MP4 `is_rap` flags against actual IDR NAL types for both normal IDR and recovery-point-SEI sources.
- Verify no sample is duplicated or omitted when the playlist transitions from parts to the completed segment URI.

### Evidence

- gohlslib v2.4.7 part finalization and independent flag: https://github.com/bluenviron/gohlslib/blob/v2.4.7/muxer_part.go
- gohlslib v2.4.7 fMP4 segment storage: https://github.com/bluenviron/gohlslib/blob/v2.4.7/muxer_segment_fmp4.go
- MP4Box.js fragmented MP4 parsing/sample extraction: https://github.com/gpac/mp4box.js/tree/v2.5.0
## 2026-11-15 — MediaMTX HLS requests share one muxer/stream reader per path

**Scope:** Exact source inspection of MediaMTX v1.21.3 `internal/servers/hls/server.go` and `muxer_instance.go`. Source-level isolation finding; no runtime load test.

### Verified behaviour

- MediaMTX HLS server maintains a muxer map keyed by path. `getOrCreateMuxer()` returns the existing muxer for that path when one is already present; it does not create a new HLS muxer for each HTTP playlist/segment/part request.
- Each `muxerInstance` creates one gohlslib muxer and one `stream.Reader`, configures the reader once with `hls.FromStream()`, registers it with the MediaMTX stream and runs it until cancellation or reader error.
- Each HTTP request is handled by the same instance's `hmuxer.Handle()` method. Additional HLS clients/worker fetches therefore share the same per-path HLS muxer and MediaMTX stream reader.
- The HLS muxer instance tracks `lastRequestTime`; the configured muxer-close-after timeout can destroy an inactive instance. A continuously active live player or replay-part fetcher keeps it active.

### Consequences for the HLS/MP4Box/WebCodecs candidate

- A dedicated replay worker fetching HLS playlists/parts does not create a separate MediaMTX `stream.Reader` per camera or per replay operation. It reads the already-produced HLS output from the path's shared muxer.
- This is a better server-side isolation boundary than opening a fresh WebRTC replay reader for every review. Replay parsing/decoding is performed in the browser worker; the MediaMTX HLS muxer continues its normal output path.
- The worker adds HTTP requests, memory copies and client-side parsing/decode CPU/GPU, so the end-to-end no-impact requirement still needs measurement. Shared muxer reuse is not proof of zero overhead.
- If the HLS worker is the only client, its ongoing playlist/part requests also keep the muxer active. If it stops and the close-after timeout expires, the HLS muxer may be destroyed and must warm up again.

### Evidence

- MediaMTX v1.21.3 per-path HLS muxer map and reuse: https://github.com/bluenviron/mediamtx/blob/v1.21.3/internal/servers/hls/server.go
- MediaMTX v1.21.3 one HLS muxer instance/one stream.Reader and shared HTTP handler: https://github.com/bluenviron/mediamtx/blob/v1.21.3/internal/servers/hls/muxer_instance.go
## 2026-11-15 — LL-HLS history retrieval: completed segments, live parts, retention and overlap rules

**Scope:** Exact source inspection of gohlslib v2.4.7 playlist generation, URI registration, segment eviction and MediaMTX v1.21.3 HLS request handling. Source-level finding; no runtime PoC.

### Verified behaviour

- In Low-Latency HLS, gohlslib v2.4.7 writes the `EXT-X-MAP` init URI into every full and delta playlist. A delta update skips old segment entries but preserves the map.
- The full media playlist lists the retained completed fMP4 segments. For the most recent two completed segments, it also lists their `EXT-X-PART` URIs. The current open segment lists its completed parts and a preload hint for the next part.
- The segment media URI is `*_seg<N>.mp4`; part URIs are `*_part<N>.mp4`. Part IDs are a separate monotonically increasing counter, not the parent segment ID plus part index.
- Each finalized part is stored and registered as its own HTTP resource. The completed segment is separately finalized, stored and registered as another HTTP resource. A completed segment contains the same stream interval as its parts; the app must not append both copies to the replay sample ring.
- On segment rotation, gohlslib appends the completed segment, registers its resource, then evicts the oldest retained segment when `len(segments) > SegmentCount`. Eviction removes the old segment URI and all part URIs belonging to that segment. Therefore a configured SegmentCount is a bounded rolling media window, not permanent archive storage.
- MediaMTX v1.21.3 routes HLS requests through gohlslib's muxer handler. Non-CDN responses are wrapped in no-cache behaviour. Low-latency playlist responses use `Cache-Control: no-cache`.
- Blocking playlist reload (`_HLS_msn` / `_HLS_part`) waits on a condition variable until the requested part exists, then returns the updated playlist. Requests outside the current media-sequence/advance-part range receive HTTP 400. A request with `_HLS_part` but no `_HLS_msn` is rejected.
- LL-HLS requires `SegmentCount >= 7` in gohlslib v2.4.7. The default is 7. The first LL-HLS completed segment also causes seven initial GAP placeholders to be inserted; those are not actual decodable media and must not be counted as valid replay history.
- Segment minimum duration is a target, not a fixed exact duration: gohlslib adjusts segmentation to include at least one IDR/random-access point. Actual retention time must be calculated from playlist EXTINF and segment timestamps, not simply `SegmentCount * 1 second`.
- The fMP4 segment is independently stored with its parts. The full segment can be fetched for older history while the most recent part resources are used for the live edge. A worker can use full segments for backfill and only ingest new parts for the currently open segment, avoiding duplicate sample insertion.

### Recommended worker retrieval algorithm

1. Fetch a non-delta full playlist (do not request `_HLS_skip`) and the current `EXT-X-MAP`. Parse `EXT-X-DISCONTINUITY-SEQUENCE`, `EXT-X-MEDIA-SEQUENCE`, `EXTINF`, `EXT-X-PROGRAM-DATE-TIME`, segment URIs, parts and gap markers.
2. At worker startup, use completed segment URIs for the retained history. Ignore `EXT-X-GAP` placeholders. Verify actual playlist duration and IDR coverage; do not assume SegmentCount equals seconds.
3. For the open/current segment, fetch each completed `EXT-X-PART` once using its unique URI/part ID. Use `_HLS_msn` + `_HLS_part` blocking reload to wait for new parts rather than polling.
4. When an open segment becomes complete, mark all its parts as superseded for ingest purposes and switch to the completed segment URI only for validation/backfill, not for appending the same media samples a second time.
5. Identify media by camera, discontinuity sequence, media sequence, URI and part index; also deduplicate samples by codec epoch plus track ID/DTS/CTS. Do not deduplicate only by timestamps because PTS can repeat/reset at discontinuities.
6. If a part/segment request returns 404/400, distinguish an expired URI, a stale preload hint, an out-of-range blocking reload and a genuine stream failure. Re-fetch a fresh full playlist and reconcile before declaring a camera fault.
7. Calculate the replay window from actual segment/part durations and timestamps. Require more than the requested 30 seconds plus safety margin so that IDR alignment, long GOPs, segment rotation and playlist update delays do not reduce usable history.

### Implications

This confirms a clean hybrid retrieval strategy for the LL-HLS → MP4Box.js → WebCodecs candidate: completed fMP4 segments provide compressed backfill; LL-HLS parts provide fresh samples near the live edge. The app must carefully avoid duplicate samples at the part-to-segment transition, ignore initial GAP placeholders, and size retention from measured durations rather than nominal segment count. No architecture is selected. Overall status remains **CONDITIONAL GO for isolated PoCs only; NO-GO for architecture lock/full implementation**.

### Evidence

- gohlslib v2.4.7 LL-HLS playlist, map, retained segment and recent-part generation: https://github.com/bluenviron/gohlslib/blob/v2.4.7/muxer_stream.go
- gohlslib v2.4.7 part URI and blocking preload resource registration: https://github.com/bluenviron/gohlslib/blob/v2.4.7/muxer_stream.go
- gohlslib v2.4.7 completed segment registration and bounded eviction: https://github.com/bluenviron/gohlslib/blob/v2.4.7/muxer_stream.go
- gohlslib v2.4.7 segment/part URI naming and LL-HLS SegmentCount minimum: https://github.com/bluenviron/gohlslib/blob/v2.4.7/muxer.go
- MediaMTX v1.21.3 HLS request routing and no-cache response handling: https://github.com/bluenviron/mediamtx/blob/v1.21.3/internal/servers/hls/muxer_instance.go
## 2026-11-15 — MediaMTX HLS muxer lifecycle: default on-demand muxer expires after 60 seconds without requests

**Scope:** Exact source inspection of MediaMTX v1.21.3 HLS server, muxer lifecycle, configuration defaults and activity timer. No runtime test or local configuration change.

### Verified behaviour

- MediaMTX v1.21.3 default `hlsAlwaysRemux` is false and default `hlsMuxerCloseAfter` is 60 seconds.
- When `hlsAlwaysRemux` is false, an HLS muxer is created on demand for a path when a client requests it. The muxer owns a `stream.Reader` and a gohlslib HLS muxer instance.
- For client-created muxers, MediaMTX checks request activity at intervals of `max(hlsMuxerCloseAfter / 3, 1 second)` and closes the muxer when the time since its last request reaches the configured close-after duration.
- Each HLS request updates `lastRequestTime`; playlist, segment, part and other media requests therefore keep the muxer alive while a player is actively consuming HLS.
- When the muxer is closed, `muxer.run()` closes its `muxerInstance`; the instance closes the gohlslib muxer and its retained segment/part storage. The in-memory rolling HLS history is therefore not persistent across an idle close.
- With `hlsAlwaysRemux` enabled and a non-source-on-demand path, MediaMTX creates an automatic muxer when the path becomes ready. The automatic muxer does not use the client inactivity timer; it remains active until the path/server lifecycle closes it.
- The HLS muxer is separate from the recorder. A closed HLS muxer does not imply the continuous recording stops, but it does mean the HLS replay history is lost and must warm up again after the next HLS request.

### Consequences for JudoReplayKit

- If HLS is used as a continuous rolling replay buffer from the start of a tournament, it cannot rely on an HLS browser tab remaining open. Either enable `hlsAlwaysRemux` for the camera paths or run a dedicated lightweight worker that continues requesting HLS often enough to prevent the 60-second inactivity close.
- `hlsAlwaysRemux` is the more deterministic option for continuous warm history but adds ongoing HLS remuxing and a reader per camera. Measure its CPU, memory, queue discards and effect on recording with both cameras active.
- If using a keep-alive worker instead, it must fetch playlists/parts reliably and account for its own failure/restart. If it stops for 60 seconds, the buffer can disappear even though MediaMTX recording continues.
- On HLS muxer recreation, the app must start a new buffer epoch, fetch the new init segment, rebuild timestamp mappings and wait for a valid IDR before declaring replay ready. It must not silently present an empty or stale history.
- Test path source-on-demand separately: `hlsAlwaysRemux` only creates automatic muxers for paths that are not configured source-on-demand.

### Required PoC tests

1. With default `hlsAlwaysRemux=false`, request HLS then stop all HLS requests for 65–90 seconds. Confirm the muxer closes, segment URIs disappear and the next client must warm up a new buffer.
2. Repeat with `hlsAlwaysRemux=true`; confirm the HLS muxer remains active and the rolling segment window continuously advances without a browser client.
3. Repeat with two camera paths, continuous MediaMTX recording and the replay worker; measure CPU, memory, HLS reader queue discards, disk writes and recording continuity.
4. Restart one camera/path and verify the HLS muxer and replay worker establish a new source/codec/time epoch without cross-epoch bookmarks.
5. Confirm the configured `hlsSegmentCount` retains the required 30-second horizon plus margin even with variable GOP/segment duration.

### Assessment

This is a material operational requirement for the HLS/WebCodecs candidate. The HLS rolling window is not a permanent or always-on buffer under MediaMTX defaults: it is demand-created and normally closed after 60 seconds without HLS requests. `hlsAlwaysRemux` can provide a continuously warm HLS history, but its two-camera resource cost must be measured. This does not invalidate the candidate, but it must be an explicit PoC gate and configuration decision. No architecture is selected. Overall status remains **CONDITIONAL GO for isolated PoCs only; NO-GO for architecture lock/full implementation**.

### Evidence

- MediaMTX v1.21.3 HLS server automatic muxer creation (`hlsAlwaysRemux`): https://github.com/bluenviron/mediamtx/blob/v1.21.3/internal/servers/hls/server.go
- MediaMTX v1.21.3 HLS muxer lifecycle, on-demand activity timer and instance recreation: https://github.com/bluenviron/mediamtx/blob/v1.21.3/internal/servers/hls/muxer.go
- MediaMTX v1.21.3 HLS muxer instance close/reader lifecycle: https://github.com/bluenviron/mediamtx/blob/v1.21.3/internal/servers/hls/muxer_instance.go
- MediaMTX v1.21.3 configuration defaults (`hlsAlwaysRemux=false`, `hlsMuxerCloseAfter=60s`): https://github.com/bluenviron/mediamtx/blob/v1.21.3/internal/conf/conf.go
## 2026-11-15 — MP4Box.js incremental sample extraction: discardMdatData, callback batching and worker memory discipline

**Scope:** Exact inspection of MP4Box.js v2.5.0 `ISOFile` implementation. Source-level integration guidance; no parser execution or real HLS fragment test.

### Verified API behaviour

- `ISOFile` constructor defaults `discardMdatData = true`. With this default, mdat payload is discarded from parser buffers unless extraction is configured and started before the relevant media data is appended. MP4Box.js explicitly warns that extraction options set while `discardMdatData` is true only extract samples whose data is appended after `start()`.
- The intended live sequence is therefore: fetch/append init segment; wait for `onReady`; identify the video track; call `setExtractionOptions(trackId, user, { nbSamples })`; call `start()`; only then append media fragments/parts. Appending the media parts before extraction is armed risks losing their sample payloads.
- `setExtractionOptions()` defaults `nbSamples` to 1000. `onSamples` is invoked when that batch count is reached or when all currently available samples have been processed. Explicitly set a small bounded batch size (e.g. one part's expected sample count) and measure callback latency; do not assume the default means one callback per HLS part.
- `appendBuffer()` requires an `ArrayBuffer` carrying a `fileStart` property and inserts it into MP4Box.js's virtual file stream. The worker must assign monotonically increasing virtual offsets for init + each appended fMP4 part/segment and preserve fragment ordering. These are parser offsets, not camera timestamps.
- `appendBuffer()` parses newly available boxes, updates sample lists after `moov` is parsed, and processes extracted samples only after `start()` has enabled `sampleProcessingStarted`.
- `onSamples` returns sample objects with their `data` arrays. The parser tracks allocated sample payload bytes and exposes `getAllocatedSampleDataSize()` and `releaseSample(trak, sampleNum)`; the worker must not leave extracted sample payloads retained indefinitely in MP4Box's internal track sample list.
- For a bounded live pipeline, copy each emitted sample's bytes into the application's own bounded compressed ring (or transfer a dedicated copy to another worker), then release parser-owned sample data as soon as ownership is safely transferred. Track MP4Box parser memory separately from ring memory. Avoid retaining duplicate full-segment and part sample payloads.
- `stop()` disables sample processing; `flush()` processes remaining samples and cleans parser buffers. Neither should be used as a per-part normal control; keep the parser running during continuous ingest and use a new parser/epoch only on a genuine discontinuity or codec/init change.

### Required implementation details

1. Set `fileStart` on each appended ArrayBuffer and maintain a single monotonic virtual byte cursor. Never reuse `fileStart=0` for each independent HLS URI within one ISOFile.
2. Append init first, arm extraction in `onReady`, call `start()`, then append media parts in strict stream order.
3. Set an explicit `nbSamples` appropriate to the fragment cadence; test both 1 and expected part sample count to find the best latency/overhead trade-off.
4. On each callback, validate track ID, sample count, sample byte length, DTS/CTS monotonicity rules and fragment continuity. Copy/transfer the bytes into the bounded ring and release MP4Box's retained sample data.
5. Measure `getAllocatedSampleDataSize()` and verify it returns to a small stable baseline after every callback. If it grows over time, fail the PoC as a memory leak/unbounded parser buffer.
6. Treat a missing sample payload, incomplete mdat, parser error or unexpected sample offset as a gap. Do not advance the bookmark/replay index across an unverified gap.

### Assessment

MP4Box.js can support a streaming extractor, but its defaults are not safe to adopt blindly for a long-running replay worker. The parser must be armed before media fragments arrive, virtual file offsets must remain monotonic, callback batch size must be explicit, and parser-owned sample bytes must be released after copying into the bounded ring. These are implementable controls, but memory stability must be demonstrated in a multi-hour two-camera soak test. No architecture is selected. Overall status remains **CONDITIONAL GO for isolated PoCs only; NO-GO for architecture lock/full implementation**.

### Evidence

- MP4Box.js v2.5.0 `ISOFile` constructor and `discardMdatData` default: https://github.com/gpac/mp4box.js/blob/v2.5.0/src/isofile.ts
- MP4Box.js v2.5.0 `setExtractionOptions`, `appendBuffer`, `onSamples`, `start`, `flush` and sample-memory release: https://github.com/gpac/mp4box.js/blob/v2.5.0/src/isofile.ts
- MP4Box.js v2.5.0 browser API and progressive parsing docs: https://github.com/gpac/mp4box.js/tree/v2.5.0
## 2026-11-15 — Correction: MP4Box.js v2.5.0 Sample uses `is_sync`, not `is_rap`; sample release index is `sample.number`

**Correction to earlier MP4Box/HLS notes:** The MP4Box.js v2.5.0 `Sample` type does not expose a property named `is_rap`. It exposes `is_sync`. The prior log entry's references to an MP4Box `is_rap` field were imprecise and should be read as the MP4 sample sync flag (`is_sync`).

### Exact source facts

- `entries/types.ts` defines `Sample` with `cts`, `dts`, `duration`, `data`, `is_sync`, `number`, `offset`, `size`, `timescale` and `track_id`; there is no `is_rap` property in that interface.
- For fragmented MP4 samples, `src/isofile.ts` sets `is_sync` from the MP4 sample flags (`sample_flags` non-sync bit). `number` is assigned as the zero-based index `trak.samples.length` when the sample is appended.
- `releaseSample(trak, sampleNum)` indexes `trak.samples[sampleNum]`. Therefore the callback's `sample.number` is the corresponding zero-based index for the fragmented sample, and is the candidate index to pass to `releaseSample` after its payload has been copied. Verify this against the actual pinned bundle and multi-part test before production use.
- The `is_sync` flag still does not prove the NAL payload contains an IDR. The earlier strict IDR rule remains: inspect AVC NAL types before using a sample as a WebCodecs `key` chunk or ring eviction point.

### Required correction to implementation notes

- Use MP4Box `sample.is_sync` when logging MP4 sync-sample metadata.
- Use `sample.number` to identify the parser's sample-list index for `releaseSample`, after validating the index against the current track sample list.
- Keep separate fields for `mp4SyncFlag`, `hasIDRNAL`, and `hasRecoveryPointSEI`; never collapse these into one `isKeyframe` boolean.

### Evidence

- MP4Box.js v2.5.0 `Sample` type: https://github.com/gpac/mp4box.js/blob/v2.5.0/entries/types.ts
- MP4Box.js v2.5.0 fragmented sample creation and `is_sync` assignment: https://github.com/gpac/mp4box.js/blob/v2.5.0/src/isofile.ts
- MP4Box.js v2.5.0 `releaseSample()` implementation: https://github.com/gpac/mp4box.js/blob/v2.5.0/src/isofile.ts
## 2026-11-15 — Hardware decode feasibility for WebCodecs on Windows/Linux

**Scope:** Review of W3C WebCodecs Working Draft dated 2026-11-02, MDN VideoDecoder/MediaCapabilities documentation, current Chromium VA-API documentation and Microsoft Edge H.264 decoder diagnostics. No device-specific test was run.

### WebCodecs API findings

- `VideoDecoderConfig.hardwareAcceleration` accepts `no-preference`, `prefer-hardware` and `prefer-software`. The W3C specification explicitly defines the latter two as hints; the browser may ignore them. A successful `VideoDecoder.isConfigSupported()` response confirms that the configuration is accepted, not that the selected decoder is hardware accelerated.
- The W3C draft recommends `no-preference` for most applications. `prefer-hardware` can restrict supported codec/profile combinations, hardware decoding may have higher startup latency, and hardware decoders may be less robust to malformed/non-conforming streams. Hardware acceleration generally reduces CPU load and can improve sustained throughput, but is not automatically faster for low-resolution streams.
- `optimizeForLatency: true` is a separate hint to reduce how many encoded chunks are needed before a decoded `VideoFrame` is output. It should be tested independently of `hardwareAcceleration`; neither hint is a guarantee.
- `VideoDecoder.isConfigSupported()` is available in Dedicated Workers in supporting browsers and returns `supported` plus the recognized config. It does not expose a definitive selected backend or hardware/software flag.
- `MediaCapabilities.decodingInfo()` returns `supported`, `smooth` and `powerEfficient` for a media-decoding configuration, but this is a browser media capability estimate for `file`, `media-source` or `webrtc` playback. It is not proof that a separately instantiated WebCodecs `VideoDecoder` uses hardware acceleration. The API may initially report smooth/power-efficient until device statistics exist.

### Platform implications

- Windows: Microsoft Edge documents a layered H.264 playback path using the Windows Media Foundation H.264 decoder with DXVA hardware acceleration, an alternative D3D11 video decode accelerator on some configurations, and a Chromium software decoder fallback. Edge's `edge://media-internals` can identify `D3D11VideoDecoder` or `MediaFoundationVideoDecoder` for a playing HTML video. This is useful platform evidence, but it does not prove that a WebCodecs `VideoDecoder` instance chooses the same backend.
- Linux: current Chromium documentation describes VA-API hardware decode as unsupported for ordinary Linux Chromium builds, with developer/test paths that may work on selected configurations and require suitable drivers/flags. Do not make Linux WebCodecs hardware acceleration a baseline assumption. Intel/AMD VA-API may be testable; NVIDIA support is specifically cautioned in Chromium docs.
- Therefore Windows hardware decode is a credible expectation to test on supported GPUs/drivers, while Linux hardware decode remains more variable and may require browser-specific setup. The application must remain correct with software decode on both platforms.

### VideoFrame memory and resource handling

- W3C defines `VideoFrame` as holding a reference to an underlying media resource, which may be backed by hardware surfaces. Transferring a VideoFrame between realms moves the resource reference and closes the source object; this can avoid copying the underlying media resource.
- This does not mean retained frames are free. A long-lived decoded-frame cache can pin hardware decoder surfaces and exhaust a decoder's surface pool or GPU memory. Always bound the active frame count and close evicted frames. Transfer frames to the UI worker only when ownership is transferred; avoid cloning unless necessary.
- If the UI must preserve many frames for exact stepping, a possible compromise is a short decoded hot window plus the compressed H.264 sample ring. Hardware decode can reduce CPU cost but cannot eliminate the memory cost of retaining many decoded frames.

### Required hardware-decode PoC matrix

1. For each real camera `avcC`/codec string and coded resolution, call `VideoDecoder.isConfigSupported()` with `no-preference`, `prefer-hardware` and `prefer-software`; record returned `supported` and recognized config. Do not infer the backend from this response.
2. Test `optimizeForLatency` true/false independently; measure configure/startup latency, keyframe-to-first-frame, steady decode throughput and frame ordering.
3. Use the same HLS/MP4Box sample stream on Windows 11 Edge and Chrome, and at least one supported Linux distribution with Chrome/Chromium and Firefox. Record browser/GPU/driver versions and H.264 profile/level.
4. On Edge/Chrome, inspect browser media diagnostics (`edge://media-internals`, `chrome://media-internals`, `edge://gpu`, `chrome://gpu`) where applicable. Treat these as corroboration for HTML playback, not definitive WebCodecs backend telemetry. Measure CPU/GPU engine utilization and power/memory during the actual WebCodecs replay worker.
5. Compare `no-preference` versus `prefer-hardware` using two cameras, two decoders, replay pause/step and a bounded VideoFrame cache. Track active VideoFrame count, cache bytes, decoder queue size, GPU memory, system RAM, CPU, first-frame latency, sustained decode rate and dropped/error frames.
6. Run with the hardware path unavailable/disabled to prove software fallback. The app must not fail or change bookmark/frame identity if hardware acceleration is absent.

### Assessment

Hardware decoding is worth testing and may materially reduce CPU pressure, especially on Windows, but it is not a safe architectural dependency. The WebCodecs API provides only a preference hint and a configuration-support check, not a guaranteed backend selector. Linux Chromium VA-API is particularly non-uniform. Keep `no-preference` as the baseline, test `prefer-hardware` as an optimization, and retain a bounded software-decode fallback. Do not count GPU memory as free storage for retained frames. No architecture is selected. Overall status remains **CONDITIONAL GO for isolated PoCs only; NO-GO for architecture lock/full implementation**.

### Evidence

- W3C WebCodecs Working Draft (2026-11-02), hardware acceleration and latency hints: https://www.w3.org/TR/2026/WD-webcodecs-20261102/
- W3C WebCodecs VideoFrame resource reference and transferable ownership: https://www.w3.org/TR/2026/WD-webcodecs-20261102/
- MDN VideoDecoder.isConfigSupported: https://developer.mozilla.org/en-US/docs/Web/API/VideoDecoder/isConfigSupported_static
- MDN MediaCapabilities.decodingInfo and powerEfficient/smooth: https://developer.mozilla.org/en-US/docs/Web/API/MediaCapabilities/decodingInfo
- Chromium VA-API documentation: https://github.com/chromium/chromium/blob/main/docs/gpu/vaapi.md
- Microsoft Edge H.264 decoder paths and diagnostics: https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/troubleshoot/microsoft-edge/development/video-playback-issues
## 2026-11-15 — LL-HLS part storage is a shared segment backing store; default storage is RAM, optional HLSDirectory uses disk

**Scope:** Exact source inspection of gohlslib v2.4.7 storage factory, file/part implementation and LL-HLS part finalization. No runtime test or local storage changes.

### Verified storage model

- gohlslib v2.4.7 chooses `storage.NewFactoryRAM()` when `Muxer.Directory` is empty and `storage.NewFactoryDisk(directory)` when it is non-empty.
- MediaMTX v1.21.3 passes `hlsDirectory` into the gohlslib muxer. The documented/default configuration sets `hlsDirectory: ""`, so the default LL-HLS rolling segment/part store is RAM-backed.
- Each LL-HLS part is created with `segment.storage.NewPart()`. The part writer writes to both the parent segment file and a per-part buffer while the segment is open. The completed segment is therefore a concatenation of its fMP4 part fragments; part URIs are views/readers of the same segment backing store, not an independent second recording.
- With disk storage, each part writes to the segment file at its offset and keeps a RAM buffer for immediate part reads. When the parent segment is finalized, the file is closed and part buffers are released; part readers then read the relevant byte range from the segment file.
- With RAM storage, the parent segment and its part data remain in memory until the segment is evicted/removed. `SegmentCount` bounds the number of segments, but actual memory use is proportional to real segment byte sizes/bitrate and variable GOP duration.
- The `hlsDirectory` disk option is not the permanent recording archive. LL-HLS playlist persistence is not written by the gohlslib `Directory` path for Low-Latency variant; it is a temporary backing store for active segment/part resources. The archive remains a separate MediaMTX recording function.

### Consequences

- If `hlsDirectory` remains empty, include the HLS ring in the RAM budget: approximately `camera bitrate × actual retained duration / 8`, plus fMP4 overhead, parser buffers, compressed replay ring (if copied), decoded cache and browser overhead.
- If a disk-backed HLS directory is considered to protect RAM, treat it as temporary working storage with bounded rolling retention, not as the tournament recording folder. It adds disk I/O and should be located on a deliberate fast local volume; measure latency and disk wear.
- The app should not store every sample twice without a reason. If MP4Box extracts from LL-HLS parts, copying compressed samples into a second application ring duplicates data that is already retained in the HLS ring. The PoC should compare: (A) retain HLS segments and parse on demand, (B) copy extracted AUs into an independent bounded ring then let HLS evict, and (C) hybrid recent sample index plus source HLS backfill.
- If HLS is the source of truth for compressed history, the worker must ensure the relevant segment URI remains retained while decoding/replay is active. A slow replay must not assume the source segment will still exist after the rolling HLS window advances; copy the required compressed samples into a separate replay-owned buffer or pin/hold the required segment data.

### Evidence

- gohlslib v2.4.7 RAM/disk storage factory selection: https://github.com/bluenviron/gohlslib/blob/v2.4.7/muxer.go
- gohlslib v2.4.7 part storage created from parent segment: https://github.com/bluenviron/gohlslib/blob/v2.4.7/muxer_stream.go
- gohlslib v2.4.7 part writer and segment backing storage: https://github.com/bluenviron/gohlslib/blob/v2.4.7/muxer_part.go
- gohlslib v2.4.7 disk file and part offset implementation: https://github.com/bluenviron/gohlslib/blob/v2.4.7/pkg/storage/file_disk.go
- gohlslib v2.4.7 disk part writer/reader: https://github.com/bluenviron/gohlslib/blob/v2.4.7/pkg/storage/part_disk.go
- MediaMTX v1.21.3 HLS directory configuration passed into muxer: https://github.com/bluenviron/mediamtx/blob/v1.21.3/internal/servers/hls/server.go
- MediaMTX default hlsDirectory: https://github.com/bluenviron/mediamtx/blob/v1.21.3/mediamtx.yml
## 2026-11-15 — LL-HLS part reads during segment finalization: concurrency stress test required

**Scope:** Exact inspection of gohlslib v2.4.7 RAM/disk storage part readers and parent segment finalization. This records a concurrency test requirement, not a confirmed upstream defect.

### Source observations

- Part HTTP handlers are registered when a part is finalized. They can remain addressable while the parent segment is still open and while subsequent parts are written.
- At parent segment rotation, the parent file's `Finalize()` finalizes storage and, in disk mode, releases the in-memory part buffers after setting the last part size and closing the file. A part request can occur near this transition.
- RAM part readers construct a reader over the part buffer; disk part readers read from the in-memory part buffer while present and use a disk-range reader after the parent file has finalized and the buffer is released.
- The inspected storage methods do not show a per-part mutex around the transition from RAM-buffer reads to disk-range reads. This is not enough by itself to conclude there is a race or functional bug; the surrounding Go concurrency model and actual race-detector results must decide.

### PoC stress test

- Repeatedly fetch every advertised part while the same segment is rotating/finalizing, in both RAM (`hlsDirectory=""`) and disk-backed modes.
- Fetch a part immediately before, during and immediately after the next segment boundary; verify HTTP status, content length, byte-for-byte consistency and parseability.
- Concurrently fetch the completed segment and verify it equals the ordered concatenation of its part payloads.
- Run a Go race-detector test against the exact pinned gohlslib version if feasible; distinguish any race in test harness from a race in the library.
- Repeat with two cameras and sustained part requests while the replay worker and live hls.js player both consume the same path.

### Assessment

This is a targeted robustness test for the HLS/WebCodecs worker because it will read parts exactly as the segment rotates. No upstream defect is asserted. The candidate remains **CONDITIONAL GO for isolated PoCs only; NO-GO for architecture lock/full implementation**.

### Evidence

- gohlslib v2.4.7 part request registration and reader callback: https://github.com/bluenviron/gohlslib/blob/v2.4.7/muxer_stream.go
- gohlslib v2.4.7 RAM part storage: https://github.com/bluenviron/gohlslib/blob/v2.4.7/pkg/storage/part_ram.go
- gohlslib v2.4.7 disk part storage and finalization: https://github.com/bluenviron/gohlslib/blob/v2.4.7/pkg/storage/part_disk.go
- gohlslib v2.4.7 disk parent file finalization: https://github.com/bluenviron/gohlslib/blob/v2.4.7/pkg/storage/file_disk.go
## 2026-11-15 — LL-HLS segment boundaries, random-access startup and codec-configuration epoch

**Scope:** Exact source inspection of the versions pinned by MediaMTX v1.21.3: gohlslib v2.4.7 and mediacommon v2.9.7. This is source-level evidence, not a camera/runtime PoC.

### Verified H.264 startup and segment behaviour

- gohlslib's H.264 segmenter does not begin writing a video track until it sees a random-access access unit. Before that point, it silently skips samples. It also initializes its H.264 DTS extractor at that first random-access point. Therefore HLS startup/readiness is dependent on the camera's actual random-access cadence; `hlsAlwaysRemux` does not make a decoder-ready first frame appear before the first usable random-access AU.
- For fMP4, a segment is rotated only when a random-access AU is encountered and the elapsed segment duration has reached `SegmentMinDuration`. The configured segment duration is a minimum/target, not a strict maximum. A long or irregular GOP can make a segment exceed the configured duration and changes the real rolling-history duration and memory/disk footprint.
- LL-HLS parts rotate on the adjusted part duration, independently of segment rotation. A part boundary is not automatically a decoder restart point.
- The pinned `mediacommon` H.264 `IsRandomAccess()` implementation treats an AU as random access when it contains an IDR NALU or an SEI recovery-point message with `recovery_frame_cnt == 0` before the first non-IDR picture. This is more precise than treating every MP4 sync flag or `EXT-X-PART:INDEPENDENT=YES` as proof of an IDR. The replay decoder must inspect the actual NAL units and retain separate IDR and recovery-point classifications.

### Codec configuration is fixed after HLS init creation

- gohlslib's H.264 segmenter updates the H.264 codec's stored SPS/PPS only while the HLS stream's init file is not yet present.
- `generateAndCacheInitFile()` creates and registers the fMP4 init resource. The inspected path does not regenerate the init resource or emit a new `EXT-X-MAP` when later in-band SPS/PPS values differ.
- Consequently, a camera changing resolution, profile, level, chroma/bit-depth or SPS/PPS during a running HLS muxer must be treated as a codec-configuration epoch change. The PoC must either prove correct new init-map/discontinuity handling or detect and reject/stop the replay epoch rather than parsing later samples against a stale `avcC`.

### Segment size and resource budget

- MediaMTX v1.21.3 defaults `hlsSegmentMaxSize` to 50 MiB and passes it to gohlslib. gohlslib counts sample payload bytes against the parent segment size; a write that would exceed the configured limit returns an error.
- This is a per-segment ceiling, not a total HLS-ring, parser, compressed-sample-cache or decoded-frame memory ceiling. With two cameras, the total resource budget still depends on actual bitrate, actual GOP-extended segment duration, retained segment count, storage mode and any duplicated replay buffers.
- The low-latency muxer requires at least seven segments and inserts seven initial GAP entries. Those entries are playlist placeholders, not decodable history. A PoC must measure the first usable media interval rather than count playlist entries.

### `hlsAlwaysRemux` readiness nuance

- MediaMTX v1.21.3 creates an automatic HLS muxer on `PathReady` only when `hlsAlwaysRemux` is true and the path is not configured `sourceOnDemand`.
- For a source-on-demand path, that automatic pre-creation branch is skipped. A muxer is instead created through the request path when a reader asks for HLS. Thus `hlsAlwaysRemux: true` alone is not proof that a source-on-demand camera's LL-HLS history is already warm before the replay request.
- The exact deployment path configuration (including `sourceOnDemand`) must therefore be included in the live-edge readiness PoC.

### Required PoC additions

1. Measure cold-start time from path ready / first HLS request to first decodable IDR and separately to first decodable recovery-point AU, for each real camera and its configured GOP.
2. Test short, normal and deliberately long GOPs. Record actual segment durations, playlist retention window, first playable timestamp and memory/segment size. Do not infer these from `hlsSegmentDuration` alone.
3. Change SPS/PPS and resolution in a controlled test stream after the HLS init resource is created. Verify whether a new init resource, `EXT-X-MAP` and discontinuity are produced. Until proven, classify such a change as a hard codec-epoch boundary.
4. Test actual IDR and recovery-point SEI samples independently with MP4Box/WebCodecs. Do not let the MP4 sync flag or HLS independent-part flag select a decoder reset point.
5. Run the same readiness test with `sourceOnDemand` both false and true; verify whether the muxer and retained HLS history exist before the first replay request.

### Assessment

This adds concrete startup, variable-GOP, codec-change and source-on-demand gates to the LL-HLS/WebCodecs PoC. It does not establish that the candidate passes those gates and does not select an architecture. Overall status remains **CONDITIONAL GO for isolated PoCs only; NO-GO for architecture lock/full implementation**.

### Evidence

- MediaMTX v1.21.3 pins gohlslib v2.4.7 and mediacommon v2.9.7: https://github.com/bluenviron/mediamtx/blob/v1.21.3/go.mod
- gohlslib v2.4.7 H.264 random-access detection, DTS initialization and fMP4 segment/part rotation: https://github.com/bluenviron/gohlslib/blob/v2.4.7/muxer_segmenter.go
- gohlslib v2.4.7 H.264 random-access and init-file SPS/PPS update gate: https://github.com/bluenviron/gohlslib/blob/v2.4.7/muxer_segmenter.go
- gohlslib v2.4.7 init-file creation, segment/part playlist generation and size handling: https://github.com/bluenviron/gohlslib/blob/v2.4.7/muxer_stream.go
- gohlslib v2.4.7 per-segment size default and seven-segment LL-HLS minimum: https://github.com/bluenviron/gohlslib/blob/v2.4.7/muxer.go
- mediacommon H.264 random-access classification (IDR or immediate recovery point): https://github.com/bluenviron/mediacommon/blob/v2.9.7/pkg/codecs/h264/is_random_access.go
- mediacommon H.264 recovery-point SEI requires `recovery_frame_cnt == 0`: https://github.com/bluenviron/mediacommon/blob/v2.9.7/pkg/codecs/h264/sei.go
- MediaMTX v1.21.3 automatic HLS muxer creation and `sourceOnDemand` condition: https://github.com/bluenviron/mediamtx/blob/v1.21.3/internal/servers/hls/server.go
- MediaMTX v1.21.3 HLS defaults including 50 MiB segment max size: https://github.com/bluenviron/mediamtx/blob/v1.21.3/internal/conf/conf.go

## 2026-11-15 — MP4Box.js v2.5.0 long-running extraction retains sample metadata

**Scope:** Exact source inspection of the tagged MP4Box.js v2.5.0 implementation. This is a source-level memory-lifecycle finding; no browser PoC or measured leak has been run.

### Verified parser memory behaviour

- `setExtractionOptions()` configures sample extraction, and `onSamples` transfers batches to the application. The extraction loop advances `trak.nextSample` and appends each extracted sample to the track's `trak.samples` array. The callback's `extractTrak.samples` batch array is cleared after callback, but the underlying per-track sample metadata array is not.
- `releaseUsedSamples(trackId, sampleNum)` loops over already-used samples and calls `releaseSample()`. `releaseSample()` subtracts the payload size from `samplesDataSize`, sets `sample.data = undefined`, resets `sample.alreadyRead`, and returns the released byte count. It does not remove the sample object from `trak.samples` or compact the array.
- `getAllocatedSampleDataSize()` reports `samplesDataSize`, which is the retained sample-payload byte count. It does not report the memory used by the growing `trak.samples` metadata array, sample objects, parsed box objects, or JavaScript object overhead.
- `stop()` only disables sample processing; `start()` re-enables it. `flush()` processes remaining samples and cleans stream buffers, but it is not a parser reset. The inspected `ISOFile` v2.5.0 class exposes no reset method that clears the accumulated track sample list.

### Consequence for a continuous tournament stream

A single MP4Box parser kept alive for hours can retain an ever-growing sample metadata history even when the application correctly calls `releaseUsedSamples()` and the reported sample-payload memory returns near zero. Therefore `getAllocatedSampleDataSize()` alone is not a sufficient long-duration memory metric and must not be treated as proof of bounded parser memory.

### Candidate containment strategies — not selected

1. **Bounded parser epochs:** create a fresh parser at controlled media boundaries (for example, per completed segment or a small group of parts), feed the matching init segment and media fragments, extract/copy samples, release payloads and dispose of the parser. Measure repeated init parsing, allocations, latency and timestamp continuity.
2. **Custom bounded sample-index lifecycle:** only if the library's internal invariants permit it, retire old sample metadata while preserving decode-order indices, fragment sequence state, timestamps and parser offsets. This is invasive and must not be attempted without a dedicated test against the pinned release.
3. **Use MP4Box only for bounded historical clip parsing:** keep a separate small hot buffer/decoder path for immediate replay and instantiate short-lived parsers for archive/segment reads.

### Required PoC additions

- Run at least a multi-hour synthetic continuous fMP4 feed at the expected camera frame rate. After every extraction callback, call `releaseUsedSamples()` correctly and record `getAllocatedSampleDataSize()`, `trak.samples.length`, parser/worker JS heap, process RAM and time-to-callback.
- Verify that sample payload bytes are released while sample metadata count continues to increase; quantify actual memory slope rather than assume a leak size.
- Compare one long-lived parser with bounded parser epochs. Verify no missing/duplicate samples at epoch boundaries, correct `tfdt`/DTS/CTS continuity, decoder start from a true IDR, and no delay in the newest replay window.
- Ensure the memory telemetry separately reports sample payload bytes, parser sample count, worker heap and decoded VideoFrame resources.

### Assessment

MP4Box.js remains plausible as a short-lived fMP4 demuxer, but a single unbounded parser instance is not yet acceptable for a tournament-long ingest worker. This is a new memory-lifecycle gate, not a confirmed runtime failure. Overall status remains **CONDITIONAL GO for isolated PoCs only; NO-GO for architecture lock/full implementation**.

### Evidence

- MP4Box.js v2.5.0 `ISOFile` extraction loop, `releaseUsedSamples()`, `releaseSample()`, `start()`, `stop()` and `flush()`: https://github.com/gpac/mp4box.js/blob/v2.5.0/src/isofile.ts
- MP4Box.js v2.5.0 package release/version: https://github.com/gpac/mp4box.js/blob/v2.5.0/package.json
- MP4Box.js memory-management guide (payload release behaviour): https://github.com/gpac/mp4box.js/wiki/Memory-managment-in-MP4Box.js

## 2026-11-15 — WebCodecs AVC key chunks require IDR; HLS recovery points are not interchangeable

**Scope:** Cross-check of MediaMTX v1.21.3 / gohlslib v2.4.7 H.264 sample flags against the W3C AVC WebCodecs registration and WebCodecs timestamp semantics. This is a standards/source compatibility finding; no browser decode PoC has been run.

### The important distinction

- MediaMTX/gohlslib marks an H.264 access unit as random access when `mediacommon` detects either an IDR NALU or an immediate recovery-point SEI (`recovery_frame_cnt == 0`). The fMP4 segmenter maps this random-access result to `IsNonSyncSample: false`.
- MP4Box.js v2.5.0 exposes the MP4 sample sync flag as `sample.is_sync`. Therefore an AU accepted by MediaMTX as a recovery point can appear as an MP4 sync sample even when it does not contain an IDR NALU.
- The W3C AVC WebCodecs registration says that an `EncodedVideoChunk` marked `type: "key"` in AVC format is expected to contain an IDR picture. In Annex-B format, a key chunk is expected to contain an IDR picture plus all parameter sets needed to decode it. Thus `sample.is_sync` or HLS `INDEPENDENT=YES` is not sufficient to label a WebCodecs AVC chunk as `key`.
- For the fMP4/AVC route, use the `avcC` record as `VideoDecoderConfig.description`, preserve the sample's length-prefixed AVC payload, and classify `EncodedVideoChunk.type` from actual NAL-unit inspection. A fresh decoder epoch must begin on a real IDR with the matching parameter sets; a recovery-point AU must not be silently promoted to a WebCodecs key chunk.

### Decode order and presentation order

- gohlslib's H.264 fMP4 segmenter extracts DTS, stores it as the sample decode time, and stores `PTS - DTS` as the composition-time offset. MP4Box exposes both `dts` and `cts` for each sample.
- WebCodecs defines `EncodedVideoChunk.timestamp` as a presentation timestamp and requires `VideoDecoder` outputs in presentation order, including internal reordering where needed.
- Therefore, if B-frames are present and the browser decoder supports the exact AVC profile/stream, the demuxer should submit samples in MP4 decode order (DTS/sample order) while assigning each chunk's timestamp from CTS/PTS converted to microseconds. Do not sort encoded chunks by CTS before calling `decode()`; that would destroy decode order.
- This makes the LL-HLS/fMP4/WebCodecs path a plausible B-frame candidate even though the MediaMTX v1.21.3 WebRTC outbound H.264 path separately rejects B-frames. Actual browser decoding and frame-order PoC remains mandatory.

### Required PoC additions

1. Build a small H.264 test set containing IDR-start GOPs, immediate recovery-point SEI GOPs, and B-frame GOPs. Inspect actual NAL units, MP4 sync flags, DTS and CTS.
2. For a fresh VideoDecoder, submit an IDR chunk marked `key` with the matching avcC description; confirm decode. Then test a recovery-point-only sample separately and confirm it is not treated as a new key chunk.
3. Feed B-frame samples in decode order with `EncodedVideoChunk.timestamp = CTS` and verify that output VideoFrames arrive in monotonically correct presentation order with the expected frame identity and timestamps.
4. Repeat with the exact camera bitstream, not only synthetic samples. Record browser, OS, GPU, codec string/profile/level and decoder errors.

### Assessment

This clarifies a critical boundary: MediaMTX's broader random-access classification is useful for segmenting, but is broader than the WebCodecs AVC `key`-chunk contract. B-frames are not automatically disqualifying for the HLS/fMP4 route, but their DTS/CTS path must be proven in the browser. No architecture is selected. Overall status remains **CONDITIONAL GO for isolated PoCs only; NO-GO for architecture lock/full implementation**.

### Evidence

- MediaMTX v1.21.3 pins gohlslib v2.4.7 and mediacommon v2.9.7: https://github.com/bluenviron/mediamtx/blob/v1.21.3/go.mod
- gohlslib v2.4.7 H.264 DTS extraction, random-access flag and fMP4 PTS offset: https://github.com/bluenviron/gohlslib/blob/v2.4.7/muxer_segmenter.go
- mediacommon v2.9.7 IDR/immediate recovery-point random-access detection: https://github.com/bluenviron/mediacommon/blob/v2.9.7/pkg/codecs/h264/is_random_access.go
- mediacommon v2.9.7 recovery-point parser: https://github.com/bluenviron/mediacommon/blob/v2.9.7/pkg/codecs/h264/sei.go
- MP4Box.js v2.5.0 MP4 sample flags and DTS/CTS fields: https://github.com/gpac/mp4box.js/blob/v2.5.0/src/isofile.ts
- W3C AVC (H.264) WebCodecs Registration, sections 2–4: https://www.w3.org/TR/webcodecs-avc-codec-registration/
- W3C WebCodecs presentation timestamp and decoder output ordering: https://www.w3.org/TR/2026/WD-webcodecs-20261102/

## 2026-11-15 — MediaMTX HLS client sessions add lightweight stream readers in addition to the shared muxer

**Scope:** Exact source inspection of MediaMTX v1.21.3 HLS session, server, muxer and stream-reader code. This is a source-level topology finding; runtime queue impact has not been measured.

### Verified reader topology

- MediaMTX keeps one HLS muxer per path and `getOrCreateMuxer()` returns the existing muxer when one already exists. The muxer instance owns its own `stream.Reader` and gohlslib HLS muxer. Individual HTTP playlist/segment/part requests are handled by that muxer; they do not each create a new HLS muxer.
- Separately, each HLS session creates a `stream.Reader` and registers a no-op callback for every original media format supported by the muxer. The source comment says this reader exists to account for outbound bytes for each HLS session, even though sessions are not directly attached to the stream through the muxer.
- Each such reader receives the stream's configured `WriteQueueSize` (default 512), owns a ring buffer and worker goroutine, and increments `OutboundFramesDiscarded` if callbacks cannot be queued. The no-op callback should be inexpensive, but a second HLS session is not literally free and adds another reader queue/worker plus per-frame callback traffic.
- HLS session lookup can reuse a session when the request carries its session token and matches the path and client IP. A new playlist request without the existing session token can create a separate HLS session even though it reuses the same path muxer.

### Consequence for live + replay consumption

- A live HLS player and a replay worker can share the same path muxer and its primary HLS conversion queue. However, if the replay worker starts a separate HLS session, MediaMTX also creates an additional lightweight accounting reader. This is smaller than a second full HLS muxer but must be included in the isolation/load test.
- Do not assume that each part GET creates a reader; the additional reader is per HLS session, not per media resource request.
- If an implementation considers reusing the live player's HLS session token for replay requests, test the exact URL/session lifecycle and IP binding. Do not assume that a token can or should be shared across workers without a deliberate design and security review.

### Required PoC additions

1. Run one live HLS client and one replay worker on the same path, first using a shared HLS session token and then using separate sessions. Inspect MediaMTX API HLS session/muxer lists, reader counts, outbound frame discards, queue behavior, CPU and memory.
2. Verify that concurrent playlist, part and segment GETs use the expected existing muxer and do not create duplicate muxers for the same path.
3. Stress the replay worker while live playback and continuous recording are active. Confirm that additional session-reader queue pressure does not cause live frame loss, recording discontinuity or unacceptable CPU/memory growth.

### Assessment

This refines the isolation model: HTTP resource requests share the path muxer, but each distinct HLS session adds a lightweight MediaMTX stream reader. It does not establish a live-impact defect; measure the actual cost. Overall status remains **CONDITIONAL GO for isolated PoCs only; NO-GO for architecture lock/full implementation**.

### Evidence

- MediaMTX v1.21.3 one-muxer-per-path lookup and muxer lifecycle: https://github.com/bluenviron/mediamtx/blob/v1.21.3/internal/servers/hls/server.go
