## 2026-11-15 — LL-HLS segment-size overflow can terminate and recreate the HLS muxer instance

**Scope:** Exact error-path tracing across gohlslib v2.4.7 and MediaMTX v1.21.3. This is a source-derived failure mode; no runtime overflow test has been run.

### Verified error propagation

- gohlslib `muxerPart.writeSample()` returns an error when adding a sample would make the parent segment exceed `SegmentMaxSize`.
- That error propagates through `fmp4WriteSample()` and `Muxer.WriteH264()` to MediaMTX's HLS `FromStream()` callback, which wraps it as `muxer error` and returns it from the stream-reader callback.
- The MediaMTX `stream.Reader` worker exits when a callback returns an error. The HLS muxer instance then removes its reader, closes the gohlslib muxer and destroys the current rolling HLS segment/part resources.
- For an automatically created HLS muxer, MediaMTX waits `recreateInstancePause` (10 seconds in v1.21.3) before attempting to create a replacement instance. This creates a potential HLS/replay history gap after a segment-size overflow. The recording path is a separate MediaMTX function and this source path does not show the recording being stopped by the HLS reader error.

### Why it matters

`hlsSegmentMaxSize` is a per-segment cap, not a graceful rolling-buffer trim. Since fMP4 segment duration can extend until the next qualifying random-access point, a long GOP combined with a high bitrate can cause a segment to exceed the size limit. The configured segment duration alone cannot prove the cap will never be reached.

### Required PoC additions

1. Use a controlled stream with a deliberately long GOP and bitrate sufficient to approach/exceed the configured segment size. Confirm exact error propagation, HLS muxer destruction/recreation timing, playlist continuity and whether recording continues uninterrupted.
2. Verify that the configured maximum leaves adequate headroom for the largest expected GOP/bitrate combination, including short-term bitrate spikes.
3. Test both automatic and on-demand HLS muxer lifecycles; distinguish the automatic 10-second recreation path from an on-demand reader/session failure.
4. Ensure the UI detects an HLS muxer restart/history gap and does not report replay availability as continuous or complete. Confirm that the live and recording health indicators remain truthful.

### Assessment

This is a concrete HLS availability risk, not evidence that ordinary camera settings will hit the limit. It reinforces the need to measure maximum observed GOP bytes and actual segment duration on the camera matrix. Overall status remains **CONDITIONAL GO for isolated PoCs only; NO-GO for architecture lock/full implementation**.

### Evidence

- gohlslib v2.4.7 parent-segment size check in part writer: https://github.com/bluenviron/gohlslib/blob/v2.4.7/muxer_part.go
- gohlslib v2.4.7 error propagation through H.264 fMP4 segmenter: https://github.com/bluenviron/gohlslib/blob/v2.4.7/muxer_segmenter.go
- gohlslib v2.4.7 `WriteH264()` returns segmenter errors: https://github.com/bluenviron/gohlslib/blob/v2.4.7/muxer.go
- MediaMTX v1.21.3 HLS `FromStream()` callbacks return muxer errors: https://github.com/bluenviron/mediamtx/blob/v1.21.3/internal/protocols/hls/from_stream.go
- MediaMTX v1.21.3 HLS muxer-instance reader error handling and destruction: https://github.com/bluenviron/mediamtx/blob/v1.21.3/internal/servers/hls/muxer_instance.go
- MediaMTX v1.21.3 automatic HLS muxer recreation pause: https://github.com/bluenviron/mediamtx/blob/v1.21.3/internal/servers/hls/muxer.go

## 2026-11-15 — Recording and HLS use separate MediaMTX stream readers; failure isolation is per consumer

**Scope:** Exact source inspection of MediaMTX v1.21.3 recorder, HLS muxer, and stream-reader lifecycle. This is a source-derived isolation finding. No runtime fault-injection or load test has been run.

### Verified architecture

- The recorder creates its own `stream.Reader` in `recorderInstance.initialize()`, registers codec-specific recording callbacks through its fMP4/MPEG-TS format implementation, and calls `Stream.AddReader()` when initialization succeeds.
- The HLS muxer instance independently creates a different `stream.Reader`, registers HLS callbacks through `protocols/hls.FromStream()`, starts its gohlslib muxer, and then calls `Stream.AddReader()`.
- These are not one shared reader or one shared callback worker. Each `stream.Reader.start()` creates its own ring buffer, worker goroutine and error channel. Each ring buffer is sized from the stream's configured `WriteQueueSize`.
- Each reader's worker executes that reader's callbacks. If one callback returns an error, that reader's worker exits and reports the error through that reader's own error channel. The HLS muxer instance responds by removing its HLS reader and closing its gohlslib muxer. The recorder instance responds by removing its recording reader and closing its recording format instance.
- The recorder's parent `Recorder.run()` uses a default two-second restart pause before creating a new recorder instance after its instance exits. The automatic HLS muxer parent uses a ten-second recreation pause after an instance crash. An HLS muxer created on demand by a specific author follows a different lifecycle: its parent returns the error instead of automatically recreating that muxer.
- The HLS muxer API aggregates its own reader's `OutboundFramesDiscarded` counter. The reader itself logs when its queue is too slow and callbacks are discarded. The recorder reader has the same queue/discard mechanism, but its per-reader discard count is not exposed as a dedicated recorder API metric in the inspected path.

### Consequence for JudoReplayKit's non-interference requirement

- A failure returned by the HLS conversion callback is handled by the HLS reader/muxer lifecycle; the source path does not directly close the recorder's separate reader. Conversely, a recording callback error is handled by the recorder instance lifecycle and does not directly close the HLS muxer reader.
- This is meaningful fault isolation, but it is **not proof of zero live impact**. All readers still consume the same underlying stream fan-out and compete for process CPU and memory. Each additional reader allocates another queue and worker. A slow reader can discard its own queued callbacks; it must not be assumed to back-pressure or preserve every frame automatically.
- The existing HLS-session accounting reader is a further, separate reader type. It must not be confused with the HLS muxer conversion reader or with the recorder reader. A design that adds a WebCodecs replay ingest reader must count that reader separately too.
- Therefore, “recording continues if HLS replay fails” is a source-supported architectural expectation, not yet an acceptance-test result. The stronger requirement—replay must not degrade live viewing or continuous recording under load—still requires measurement on the actual PC and camera streams.

### Required isolated PoC tests

1. Run one camera with continuous recording and one live HLS player. Capture baseline CPU, process RAM, recording segment cadence, HLS part cadence, and all available reader/muxer discard counters.
2. Add the proposed replay consumer without changing the live or recording configuration. Verify whether it creates a new stream reader, a new HLS session reader, or both. Record the additional queue/worker count and memory.
3. Inject a controlled HLS callback/muxer error. Confirm that the HLS instance closes/recreates according to its actual automatic/on-demand lifecycle while recording segments continue without a missing interval.
4. Inject a controlled recording callback error. Confirm the recorder's own two-second restart path and verify that the live HLS player remains active. Mark the recording gap explicitly; do not report it as uninterrupted.
5. Saturate one reader queue deliberately. Confirm that its discard counter/log changes while other readers continue receiving frames; measure whether CPU contention or shared fan-out still affects their latency.
6. Repeat with two cameras, replay scrubbing, pause/frame-step, and simultaneous continuous recording. Check disk writes, process RAM, queue discards, timestamps, and recording continuity over a multi-hour run.

### Assessment

The source confirms separate reader queues and separate failure/restart lifecycles for recording and HLS conversion. This supports the intended isolation boundary, but it does not establish performance isolation under contention and does not satisfy the live non-interference acceptance test by itself. Status remains **CONDITIONAL GO for isolated PoCs only; NO-GO for architecture lock/full implementation**.

### Evidence

- MediaMTX v1.21.3 recorder reader creation, registration and AddReader: https://github.com/bluenviron/mediamtx/blob/v1.21.3/internal/recorder/recorder_instance.go
- MediaMTX v1.21.3 recorder restart lifecycle and two-second default pause: https://github.com/bluenviron/mediamtx/blob/v1.21.3/internal/recorder/recorder.go
- MediaMTX v1.21.3 fMP4 recording callbacks and per-format reader registration: https://github.com/bluenviron/mediamtx/blob/v1.21.3/internal/recorder/format_fmp4.go
- MediaMTX v1.21.3 HLS muxer reader creation, registration and instance teardown: https://github.com/bluenviron/mediamtx/blob/v1.21.3/internal/servers/hls/muxer_instance.go
- MediaMTX v1.21.3 HLS automatic/on-demand muxer lifecycle and ten-second recreation pause: https://github.com/bluenviron/mediamtx/blob/v1.21.3/internal/servers/hls/muxer.go
- MediaMTX v1.21.3 stream reader ring buffer, callback worker, per-reader error channel and discard counter: https://github.com/bluenviron/mediamtx/blob/v1.21.3/internal/stream/reader.go
- MediaMTX v1.21.3 stream AddReader/RemoveReader and reader fan-out registration: https://github.com/bluenviron/mediamtx/blob/v1.21.3/internal/stream/stream.go

## 2026-11-15 — MediaMTX fans out each media unit to independent reader queues without waiting for each callback

**Scope:** Exact source inspection of MediaMTX v1.21.3 stream fan-out and reader queue implementation, cross-checked against the recorder and HLS callback registration. Source-level result only; no stress test or race-detector run has been performed.

### Verified fan-out behaviour

- SubStreamFormat.writeUnitInner() performs the stream-level transformations first: timestamp handling, format update, unit remuxing and any required RTP encoding. It then iterates the registered onDatas readers for that format.
- For each registered reader, it creates a callback closure and calls that reader's push() method. Reader.push() attempts to enqueue the callback into that reader's own ring buffer. If the buffer is full, that reader's discard counter is incremented; the push does not wait for the callback to finish.
- The callback worker executes asynchronously in Reader.runInner(). A callback error terminates that reader's worker and is reported through that reader's error channel. It is not returned synchronously from the callback worker to the source's SubStreamFormat.writeUnitInner() call.
- The recorder and HLS muxer therefore receive callbacks through separate queues. A slow or failed reader has its own queue/discard/error lifecycle; the producer continues iterating over the other registered readers.
- The callback closures capture the same *unit.Unit pointer for a given incoming unit. The built-in HLS callback passes its fields/payload to gohlslib, and the fMP4 recorder callback constructs recording samples from the same unit. The inspected call sites do not intentionally rewrite the unit.Unit fields after fan-out. Nevertheless, the shared pointer and payload slices make immutability an important invariant for any new replay callback.

### What this proves—and what it does not

- **Proven at source level:** queue capacity and callback execution are per reader; a full reader queue increments that reader's discard counter rather than blocking on that callback; callback errors are isolated to the reader worker's error channel.
- **Not proven:** zero CPU interference, zero allocation/GC impact, zero lock contention, zero effect from the producer's fan-out loop, or guaranteed recording/live continuity under sustained load. Every incoming unit still incurs fan-out work for every registered reader, and each reader's queued closures retain references to the unit/payload until executed or discarded.
- **Important implementation constraint:** a custom WebCodecs replay consumer must treat each received unit and its payload as immutable, or make a deliberate copy before mutation. It must also close/release every decoded VideoFrame and bound its own queue; otherwise it can create memory pressure even though MediaMTX's queue is independent.
- This source inspection supports the conclusion that reader isolation is a real queue/error boundary, but it is not a substitute for measuring the complete application under two-camera load.

### Required PoC tests

1. Instrument one source unit with sequence/timestamp identity and verify that the recorder, HLS muxer and a test replay reader each receive the same ordered identities when all queues remain below capacity.
2. Artificially slow only the replay reader until its ring buffer fills. Confirm its discard counter increases while recorder and HLS callbacks continue; compare source frame rate, live output, recording sample count and file growth.
3. Artificially slow the recorder reader separately. Verify HLS/live continuity and record the expected recorder restart/gap behaviour if its callback itself returns an error.
4. Run the Go race detector with concurrent recorder, HLS and replay callbacks. Include a test callback that attempts mutation only in an isolated test harness to confirm the shared-unit hazard is detected; production callbacks must remain read-only.
5. Measure process CPU, heap, allocation rate, GC pauses and queue discards as each additional reader is enabled. Repeat with both cameras and a multi-hour soak.
6. Verify that queue-full discard telemetry is surfaced honestly in the JudoReplayKit health UI; a green “recording” lamp must not imply that every frame was persisted if the recorder reader has discarded callbacks.

### Assessment

MediaMTX's fan-out design provides per-reader queue and callback-error isolation and does not synchronously wait for each reader callback. This is a strong architectural basis for independent replay, but resource contention and the shared-unit immutability invariant remain explicit test gates. Status remains **CONDITIONAL GO for isolated PoCs only; NO-GO for architecture lock/full implementation**.

### Evidence

- MediaMTX v1.21.3 per-format fan-out loop and asynchronous callback enqueue: https://github.com/bluenviron/mediamtx/blob/v1.21.3/internal/stream/sub_stream_format.go
- MediaMTX v1.21.3 reader ring buffer, worker, error channel and per-reader discard counter: https://github.com/bluenviron/mediamtx/blob/v1.21.3/internal/stream/reader.go
- MediaMTX v1.21.3 stream reader registration/removal: https://github.com/bluenviron/mediamtx/blob/v1.21.3/internal/stream/stream.go
- MediaMTX v1.21.3 recorder callback registration: https://github.com/bluenviron/mediamtx/blob/v1.21.3/internal/recorder/format_fmp4.go
- MediaMTX v1.21.3 HLS callback registration: https://github.com/bluenviron/mediamtx/blob/v1.21.3/internal/protocols/hls/from_stream.go

## 2026-11-15 — Additive correction: gohlslib v2.4.7 disk-backed part reads have an unsynchronized finalization race

**Source:** Follow-up exact source trace of gohlslib v2.4.7 after the earlier LL-HLS part-rotation note. This entry adds a stronger finding; it does not delete or rewrite the earlier, more cautious assessment. No Go race-detector run has yet been performed.

### Exact source path

- A completed LL-HLS part is registered with an HTTP handler in muxer_stream.go. That handler calls part.reader() directly; it does not acquire the muxerStream mutex before doing so.
- For disk-backed storage, partDisk.Reader() checks p.buffer. If it is non-nil, it calls p.buffer.Bytes() and returns a reader over the in-memory bytes. If it is nil, it calls newDiskPartReader() using p.offset and p.size.
- When the parent fMP4 segment is finalized, muxerSegmentFMP4.finalize() calls fileDisk.Finalize(). That method sets the last part's size, then iterates through the parts and assigns p.buffer = nil before closing the parent file.
- The parent segment rotation is protected by the gohlslib muxer mutex, but the already-registered part HTTP handler does not acquire that mutex when it calls part.reader(). The fileDisk.Finalize()/partDisk.Reader() transition therefore has no shared lock protecting the buffer pointer and size fields.
- In particular, partDisk.Reader() reads p.buffer once for the nil check and again for p.buffer.Bytes(). Concurrently assigning p.buffer = nil is an unsynchronized read/write of the pointer. The final-part p.size field is also written during parent finalization and can be read by the disk-range reader without a shared synchronization mechanism.

### Assessment

This is stronger than a generic “possible race” based only on the absence of an obvious part-level mutex: the exact reader and finalizer code access the same mutable fields without a common lock. It is a **source-derived data-race candidate** for disk-backed LL-HLS part requests that overlap parent-segment finalization. Depending on timing, the pointer transition may produce a race-detector report and may allow a nil dereference or an incorrect disk-range size. This is not yet a reproduced crash, corrupted response, or confirmed upstream bug; runtime proof is still required.

The earlier RAM-vs-disk distinction remains important:
- RAM-backed part storage does not perform the same parent-finalization buffer-nil transition.
- Disk-backed storage uses the dual RAM/disk part reader and is the specific path implicated here.
- The race window concerns a part that has already been finalized and made addressable, while its parent segment is subsequently being finalized.

### Required verification and remediation gate

1. Build a Go test against the exact pinned gohlslib v2.4.7 source with disk-backed storage.
2. Repeatedly request the final part of a segment while forcing the parent segment to rotate/finalize. Run the test with the Go race detector; retain the full race report and exact goroutine stacks.
3. Verify HTTP status, response length, MP4 parseability and byte-for-byte equality against a stable copy. Include requests immediately before, during and after parent finalization.
4. Repeat the same stress test with RAM-backed storage as a control.
5. If reproduced, fix synchronization at the storage abstraction boundary (or take a safe immutable snapshot of the reader state) and add a regression test. Do not patch around it only in JudoReplayKit's fetch loop, because the mutable state is inside the library.
6. Re-run the two-camera live + recording + replay load test after any upstream/library patch.

### Evidence

- gohlslib v2.4.7 part HTTP handler calls part.reader() without taking muxerStream.mutex: https://github.com/bluenviron/gohlslib/blob/v2.4.7/muxer_stream.go
- gohlslib v2.4.7 partDisk.Reader() reads buffer and falls back to disk using offset/size: https://github.com/bluenviron/gohlslib/blob/v2.4.7/pkg/storage/part_disk.go
- gohlslib v2.4.7 fileDisk.Finalize() writes final part size, nils part buffers and closes the file: https://github.com/bluenviron/gohlslib/blob/v2.4.7/pkg/storage/file_disk.go
- gohlslib v2.4.7 parent segment finalization calls storage.Finalize(): https://github.com/bluenviron/gohlslib/blob/v2.4.7/muxer_segment_fmp4.go
- gohlslib v2.4.7 segment rotation and muxer-level synchronization: https://github.com/bluenviron/gohlslib/blob/v2.4.7/muxer.go

## 2026-11-15 — Critical replay-latency finding: Rev.8 loads the entire recording span before seeking

**Scope:** Cross-check of the archived Rev.8 app.js snapshot, Rev.8 MediaMTX test configuration, and MediaMTX v1.21.3 Playback GET implementation. This is a source-derived design/performance finding; no new playback benchmark has been run.

### Exact Rev.8 application behaviour

- In the archived Rev.8 app.js snapshot, loadSpan() sets the replay video source to playbackUrl(STREAMS[i], span.start, span.end - span.start). It therefore requests the entire archive span returned by the playback list, not just the 10–30 second replay window.
- Only after the video element reaches canplay does loadSpan() set video.currentTime to the requested absolute replay position relative to the beginning of that span.
- The Rev.8 test configuration sets recordSegmentDuration to 1h. A single listed span can therefore cover close to an hour. Instant Replay can request that entire span for each camera and then seek near its end.
- The same test configuration sets recordDeleteAfter to 24h. That is a test value, not a confirmed final retention decision; if the product target remains 30 days, this test value does not meet it.

### MediaMTX v1.21.3 Playback GET behaviour

- Playback GET explicitly sends Accept-Ranges: none. The endpoint does not support byte-range seeking.
- The server's seekAndMux() opens the first selected recording file and passes it to segmentFMP4MuxParts(). That function parses the fMP4 boxes and walks the sample entries in file order.
- For each sample, the fMP4 playback muxer invokes the payload reader before deciding whether the sample is before the requested start. Samples before the requested start are used to find/retain the relevant GOP; their payloads are still read. Consequently, requesting a short clip near the end of a long recording file can still require work proportional to the amount of media before the requested time.
- The GET response is a newly muxed fMP4 stream for the requested start/duration. It is not a byte-range slice of the original file.

### Why this conflicts with immediate referee replay

The current Rev.8 path has two avoidable costs:
1. The browser is asked to load the whole listed span (potentially almost one hour) even though the UI only needs a short review window.
2. Even if the browser request is narrowed to the review window, MediaMTX v1.21.3 still scans and reads earlier samples in the selected recording file to locate the correct GOP. A one-hour recording segment can therefore still impose a long seek-to-target delay.

This is a concrete architectural explanation for why the current archive-based implementation may fail the “immediate access” requirement. It is not yet a measured explanation of the user's earlier black replay window; decoding, MP4 compatibility, URL routing and browser behaviour remain separate checks.

### Required repair direction — do not apply to production

1. Change the replay request to ask for only the required time window plus a deliberate decode preroll/keyframe allowance, rather than span.start through span.end. Keep the UI's absolute-time mapping explicit and test that the first displayed frame is the intended frame.
2. Do not assume request clipping alone solves latency. Benchmark the server-side scan cost with the current one-hour recording segments.
3. Evaluate shorter recording segments or a separately indexed rolling replay cache so a requested moment is located without scanning an hour of samples. Choose segment length only after measuring actual GOP duration, keyframe interval, file count, storage overhead and seek latency.
4. Test the first replay frame latency at several positions within a segment (near its start, midpoint and end), with recording continuing. Record server CPU, bytes returned, bytes read if instrumented, and time to first decoded frame.
5. Keep continuous recording independent. Any cache/index must be disposable/rebuildable and must not become the only copy of the match.
6. Review the test retention setting separately. Do not silently change the production retention or delete policy.

### Acceptance criteria

- A 15-second instant replay request must not download a whole one-hour recording span.
- Time-to-first-decoded-frame must remain within the agreed referee-use target even when the requested moment is near the end of a recording segment.
- Replay opening, seeking, pausing, frame stepping and closing must not interrupt live playback or continuous recording.
- Test at least one full hour of recording and both cameras before approving the selected segment/index strategy.

### Evidence

- Archived Rev.8 app.js snapshot: 02_CURRENT_APP_JS.txt, loadSpan() at line 504 calls playbackUrl() with the complete span start and duration; the video seek occurs only after waitForVideo()/canplay.
- Archived Rev.8 test config: mediamtx-runtime.yml, recordSegmentDuration: 1h and recordDeleteAfter: 24h at line 35.
- MediaMTX v1.21.3 Playback GET sets Accept-Ranges: none and calls seekAndMux(): https://github.com/bluenviron/mediamtx/blob/v1.21.3/internal/playback/on_get.go
- MediaMTX v1.21.3 fMP4 playback parser walks sample entries and invokes the payload reader: https://github.com/bluenviron/mediamtx/blob/v1.21.3/internal/playback/segment_fmp4.go
- MediaMTX v1.21.3 fMP4 playback muxer reads sample payloads before dropping pre-start samples and retains the GOP: https://github.com/bluenviron/mediamtx/blob/v1.21.3/internal/playback/muxer_fmp4.go

## 2026-11-15 — Rev.8 route clarification and LL-HLS disk-race applicability

**Scope:** Cross-check of the archived Rev.8 app.js, Caddyfile and MediaMTX test configuration against the earlier playback-route and LL-HLS storage findings.

### Playback URL mismatch candidate — closed for the archived Rev.8 app.js

- The earlier playback-list response was observed to include a generated URL using the unprefixed /get path, while Caddy's configured playback reverse-proxy route is /playback/* and strips the /playback prefix before forwarding to MediaMTX.
- The archived Rev.8 app.js does not consume the generated URL field from the list response. It constructs its own URL in playbackUrl(), using /playback/get with path, start, duration and format=fmp4.
- Caddy's /playback/* route strips /playback and forwards /get to localhost:9995, which is the correct MediaMTX Playback GET route.
- Therefore, the list response's unprefixed generated URL is **not by itself the cause** of the archived Rev.8 app's black replay window. Keep the generated URL mismatch as an API-response observation, but do not change Caddy routing on that basis. The dominant source-level issue found in this review is the whole-span request and subsequent seek described in the preceding entry.

### LL-HLS disk-backed part race — not active in the archived Rev.8 test configuration

- The archived Rev.8 MediaMTX test configuration sets hlsVariant to mpegts and does not set hlsDirectory.
- The disk-backed part finalization race recorded above is specific to the gohlslib fMP4/Low-Latency HLS part path when disk-backed HLS storage is selected. It is not exercised by the archived Rev.8 MPEG-TS HLS configuration.
- The finding remains relevant only if a future configuration switches to Low-Latency HLS and supplies a non-empty HLS directory. It must not be treated as a current Rev.8 defect or used as a reason to change the existing test HLS variant.

### Evidence

- Archived Rev.8 app.js, playbackUrl() at line 276 constructs /playback/get: 02_CURRENT_APP_JS.txt.
- Archived Rev.8 app.js, loadSpan() at line 504 uses the constructed URL: 02_CURRENT_APP_JS.txt.
- Archived Rev.8 Caddyfile routes /playback/*, strips the prefix and proxies to localhost:9995: 01_SUPPORTING_FILES.txt, app/Caddyfile.
- Archived Rev.8 MediaMTX config selects hlsVariant: mpegts and leaves hlsDirectory unset: 01_SUPPORTING_FILES.txt, app/mediamtx-runtime.yml.
- MediaMTX v1.21.3 Playback GET handler: https://github.com/bluenviron/mediamtx/blob/v1.21.3/internal/playback/on_get.go
- gohlslib v2.4.7 fMP4 part storage implementation: https://github.com/bluenviron/gohlslib/tree/v2.4.7/pkg/storage

## 2026-11-15 — Rev.8 bookmark recording-time calibration can drift while the description prompt is open

**Scope:** Exact review of the archived Rev.8 app.js bookmark path. This is a source-derived timing defect; it has not yet been reproduced in a live camera test.

### Verified code sequence

- The bookmark click handler captures clickTime = Date.now() before showing the blocking prompt() for the bookmark description.
- After the user dismisses the prompt and the bookmark is saved, the code calls fetchLatestRecordingEnd().
- fetchLatestRecordingEnd() independently samples a new Date.now(), then asks the archive list for spans from that later time minus 30 seconds through that later time plus 2 seconds. It returns the latest span end from that later query.
- The result is stored as lastSpanEnd, and recordingLag is calculated as the original clickTime minus that later latestEnd.
- openBookmarkReplay() uses lastSpanEnd as its replay target whenever it exists, rather than the original clickTime adjusted by a stable recording-clock calibration.

### Failure mode

Because prompt() blocks the page while the user types, the archive query is performed after the original bookmark click. If the user spends several seconds entering a description, the latest recording end can advance by those seconds. The stored lastSpanEnd can therefore refer to a later moment than the actual button click, and the bookmark replay target can drift forward by approximately the time spent in the prompt (plus recording/query timing). A negative recordingLag is possible and should not be silently treated as a normal positive recording delay.

This makes bookmark placement dependent on how quickly the official types the description, which is undesirable for a referee event marker.

### Required correction direction

1. Preserve the original clickTime as the immutable event timestamp.
2. Calibrate the archive time against that clickTime, not against a fresh Date.now() taken after the prompt. Query a bounded interval around clickTime and select the closest valid recording timestamp using an explicit tolerance.
3. Keep the click timestamp and recording timestamp as separate fields. Preserve signed lag and flag implausible values rather than shifting the bookmark silently.
4. If calibration is unavailable, keep the exact clickTime and mark the recording position as approximate; do not substitute the newest later archive time.
5. Add tests with prompt delays of 0, 5, 15 and 30 seconds and with simulated recorder lag. The replay target should remain tied to the original click within the defined tolerance.
6. The same handler currently expands the bookmark drawer immediately after saving. This conflicts with the recorded product decision that adding a bookmark must not automatically open the drawer. Keep that known UI defect separate from the timestamp correction.

### Evidence

- Archived Rev.8 app.js captures clickTime before prompt(): 02_CURRENT_APP_JS.txt, line 1186.
- Archived Rev.8 app.js fetchLatestRecordingEnd() samples a fresh Date.now() and queries a rolling 30-second window: 02_CURRENT_APP_JS.txt, line 1142.
- Archived Rev.8 app.js saves the later latestEnd as lastSpanEnd and computes recordingLag against the earlier clickTime: 02_CURRENT_APP_JS.txt, line 1202.
- Archived Rev.8 app.js opens the drawer after bookmark creation: 02_CURRENT_APP_JS.txt, line 1199.
- Archived Rev.8 app.js uses lastSpanEnd as the bookmark replay target: 02_CURRENT_APP_JS.txt, line 776.


## 2026-11-16 — Rev.9 PoC 1 package review: Preflight and Tool Setup v0.1

**Source:** Static review of the two user-supplied ZIP packages:
- `JudoReplayKit_Rev9_PoC1_Preflight_v0.1(1).zip`
- `JudoReplayKit_Rev9_PoC1_Tool_Setup_v0.1(1).zip`

**Review status:** Source code inspected; neither PowerShell script has been executed in this review. No user PC, Rev.9 workspace, Rev.8 installation, production installation, processes, ports, or recordings were accessed or changed.

### 1. Preflight package — conditionally approved for the isolated inventory step

The README and `00-PoC1-Preflight.ps1` agree on the following scope:
- The root is hard-coded as `C:\JudoReplayKit_Rev9_Dev`.
- The script refuses to continue if that directory is missing, is a reparse point, or resolves to a different path.
- Its only intended write is a timestamped report under `C:\JudoReplayKit_Rev9_Dev\PoC1\logs\`.
- It does not start, stop, restart, kill or configure services/processes; it does not read media contents or configuration contents; it does not intentionally access the known production or Rev.8 paths.
- It inventories workspace files, selected executable/config names, PATH availability, matching running process metadata, selected TCP listeners and local fixed-disk free space.

No source-level command was found that targets `D:\JudoReplayKit` or `D:\JudoReplayKit_Rev8_Test`. The script is therefore suitable for the next read-only preflight step, provided the user confirms that the isolated Rev.9 workspace already exists at the exact C: path. It must not be redirected to another folder.

**Privacy note:** The report includes computer name, Windows username, process names/IDs/start times, local file paths and disk information. The README correctly instructs the user to inspect and redact the report before sharing it.

**Limitations:** This is an inventory, not a functional validation. A process or listening port does not prove that a stream, recording or replay works. Some CIM/network inventory commands can fail due to system policy; the script has limited handling for the TCP-listener query and may stop on other inventory errors. That is acceptable for a preflight, but an incomplete report must be treated as incomplete rather than as a pass.

### 2. Tool Setup package — not approved to run yet

The script is confined to `C:\JudoReplayKit_Rev9_Dev\PoC1\tools`, refuses to proceed if that tools directory already exists, does not modify PATH or registry, and does not start MediaMTX or open ports. It downloads:
- MediaMTX v1.21.3 Windows amd64 from the official `bluenviron/mediamtx` GitHub release.
- A pinned FFmpeg-Builds Windows x86_64 LGPL-labelled static archive from the `autobuild-2026-11-14-12-37` release.

The MediaMTX v1.21.3 release exists, and the exact Windows amd64 archive name used by the script is consistent with the official release naming. The official MediaMTX release documentation describes checksum verification and GitHub Attestations; the setup script currently downloads the checksum manifest over HTTPS and compares SHA-256, but does not verify the GitHub Attestation. The FFmpeg-Builds release page confirms the named 9.0 build and LGPL static archive. These external checks confirm release/asset identity, not the actual bytes downloaded on the user's PC.

#### Issues to fix before execution

1. **Partial setup blocks retry.** The script creates the tools directory before downloads and extraction. If any download, manifest lookup, checksum comparison, extraction or executable check fails, the catch block exits but leaves the tools directory and partial files in place. A second run then refuses because the directory exists. Add a safe failure strategy: stage downloads/extraction in a unique temporary directory inside the approved PoC1 workspace, verify everything, then promote to `tools` only when complete; never delete or overwrite an existing directory. If a failed staging folder is retained for diagnosis, report its exact path and how to inspect it.
2. **Checksum provenance is limited.** The archive and checksum manifest are fetched from the same release host/repository. This detects transfer corruption and mismatched files, but is not independent publisher authentication. For MediaMTX, prefer verifying the published GitHub Attestation (or compare against an independently pinned, previously verified digest) in addition to the checksum file. Do not imply that the current manifest comparison alone proves publisher identity.
3. **FFmpeg licensing label is not fully established by the current check.** The script rejects build configuration strings containing `--enable-gpl` or `--enable-nonfree`, which is a useful guard, but this alone is not a complete legal audit of every linked component or redistribution obligation. Preserve the exact build identity and build configuration, and retain the upstream license/notices with the test tools. Do not describe the binary as legally verified solely because those two flags are absent.
4. **No post-failure cleanup/reporting strategy.** In addition to the staging issue, the current catch path does not clearly distinguish a failed download, failed checksum, failed extraction or failed executable validation in a durable summary beyond the console/log message. Improve the log so it records the stage and exact failing file without deleting evidence.
5. **No tool execution has been authorized by this review.** The setup script is not a server launcher, but it does download and extract large executable archives and run their version/build-information commands. It must remain a separate step after the preflight report is inspected.

### 3. Decision and next sequence

- **Preflight v0.1:** Conditional GO for a read-only run against the already-existing `C:\JudoReplayKit_Rev9_Dev` directory only. Inspect/redact its report before sharing.
- **Tool Setup v0.1:** NO-GO pending the staging/retry fix and stronger provenance handling described above.
- **PoC 1 live-edge test:** Not ready. Do not start MediaMTX, send synthetic streams, edit configuration, or test against production/Rev.8 as part of these package steps.

The product acceptance basis remains unchanged: replay must show the actually available recent interval while the recording segment is still being written; continuous recording must keep progressing on both cameras; replay must not interrupt live output or recording. A successful download, HTTP response, process state or file-size increase alone is not a PoC PASS.

### Evidence and external source checks

- MediaMTX v1.21.3 release and release notes: https://github.com/bluenviron/mediamtx/releases
- MediaMTX official binary security and GitHub Attestation guidance: https://mediamtx.org/docs/misc/security
- FFmpeg-Builds release page showing the pinned 2026-11-14 12:37 release and the `n9.0.2-57-gd838f9f9ae` LGPL static Windows archive: https://github.com/FFmpeg-Builds/FFmpeg-Builds/releases
- MediaMTX v1.21.3 Windows archive identity cross-check (third-party Scoop manifest): https://github.com/ScoopInstaller/Main/blob/master/bucket/mediamtx.json

## 2026-11-16 — Follow-up provenance checks for Tool Setup v0.2

**Source:** Official MediaMTX security/release documentation and public GitHub release metadata checked during the continuation review.

- MediaMTX's official security page explicitly recommends verifying each released binary with GitHub CLI `gh attestation verify --repo bluenviron/mediamtx`, in addition to checking the downloaded file against the release's `checksums.sha256` manifest.
- The v1.21.3 release page identifies the release and its release workflow. A third-party Scoop manifest independently lists the Windows amd64 archive URL and SHA-256 `b2f5246b07cb72961df03b8d5e8cad43dc657bcf3f49a87bb031bdf22721af74`. This is useful as a cross-check, but the upstream GitHub Attestation remains the preferred provenance proof.
- GitHub CLI documents `gh attestation verify <file> --repo <owner/repo>` as validating signed attestation identity and predicate type. Tool Setup v0.2 should either require an already installed, functioning `gh` command and verify the MediaMTX archive before extraction, or explicitly stop with a clear prerequisite message. It must not silently downgrade to checksum-only verification while claiming attestation validation.
- The pinned FFmpeg-Builds release page confirms the exact 2026-11-14 12:37 release and the selected Windows x86_64 LGPL-labelled static archive identity. The available page evidence does not, by itself, establish a separate independent digest or complete licensing audit. Preserve its release URL, exact asset name, archive SHA-256, build configuration output and any included license/notices. Report checksum provenance honestly and do not call the binary legally verified based only on absence of GPL/nonfree flags.

### Updated Tool Setup v0.2 requirements

1. Keep the fixed workspace root `C:\\JudoReplayKit_Rev9_Dev`; refuse reparse-point or path redirection.
2. Create a unique staging directory under `C:\\JudoReplayKit_Rev9_Dev\\PoC1` (never beside production or Rev.8). Do not create the final `tools` directory until every required archive passes verification and extraction checks.
3. If a final `PoC1\\tools` directory already exists, stop without modifying it. If staging fails, preserve the stage directory and write a durable error report naming the exact stage, URL, file and exception; do not delete evidence or leave a partial final tools directory.
4. Verify MediaMTX using both the upstream release checksum manifest and GitHub Attestation where `gh` is available; otherwise stop before installing it. Record the third-party pinned digest only as an additional cross-check, not as a replacement for the attestation.
5. Pin FFmpeg to the exact reviewed release and asset; verify its SHA-256 against the selected release manifest/source and record provenance limitations and build configuration. Retain upstream notices/license files.
6. Extract into staging, verify the expected executable paths, capture version/build configuration, and only then promote the complete staging payload to `PoC1\\tools` with a same-volume rename that fails if the destination exists.
7. Do not add PATH entries, registry keys, services, firewall rules or scheduled tasks. Do not launch MediaMTX or open listeners.
8. Treat this as tool preparation only. It does not authorize synthetic streams, live-edge tests, configuration edits or any contact with production/Rev.8.

**Decision remains:** Tool Setup v0.1 is NO-GO. The user has not been instructed to run it. Tool Setup v0.2 must be prepared and reviewed as a separate revision before execution.

## 2026-11-16 — Direct source review of Tool Setup v0.1 script

**Source:** Complete PowerShell source pasted by the user from:
`C:\\Users\\otval\\Downloads\\JudoReplayKit_Rev9_PoC1_Tool_Setup_v0.1\\scripts\\01-Download-PoC1-Tools.ps1`.
**Review type:** Static source review only. The script was not executed. No downloads, extraction, executable invocation, service changes, configuration changes or access to production/Rev.8 occurred.

### Confirmed script behavior

- Hard-codes the Rev.9 workspace as `C:\\JudoReplayKit_Rev9_Dev`.
- Checks that the workspace exists and is not itself a reparse point.
- Refuses to proceed if `PoC1\\tools` already exists.
- Creates `PoC1\\tools`, `tools\\downloads`, and the log directory before attempting downloads.
- Downloads MediaMTX v1.21.3 Windows amd64 archive and its `checksums.sha256` from the official GitHub release.
- Downloads the pinned FFmpeg-Builds archive `ffmpeg-n9.0.2-57-gd838f9f9ae-win64-lgpl-9.0.zip` from tag `autobuild-2026-11-14-12-37`, plus a file named `checksums.sha256`.
- Searches each manifest for a line containing the expected asset name, parses either a 64-character SHA-256 line or a `SHA256 (file) = hash` line, then compares the local archive hash.
- Extracts both archives into the final `tools` tree, recursively searches for the executables, invokes MediaMTX `--version`, FFmpeg `-version` and `-buildconf`, and saves the FFmpeg build configuration.
- Rejects a build configuration containing `--enable-gpl` or `--enable-nonfree`.
- Does not intentionally start a MediaMTX server, create a stream, add firewall rules, edit PATH or registry, or create a service.

### Confirmed NO-GO reasons

1. **Final destination is created too early.** The script creates `PoC1\\tools` before any download or verification. Any failure leaves a partial final directory; later runs refuse because it exists. This is the primary retry/atomicity defect.
2. **No staging/promotion boundary.** Downloads, extraction and validation all occur inside the final destination. A complete verified payload is not promoted atomically.
3. **No GitHub Attestation verification.** MediaMTX checksum-manifest comparison alone does not implement the upstream-recommended attestation check. The v0.2 design must call `gh attestation verify <archive> --repo bluenviron/mediamtx` and fail closed if it fails.
4. **Failure reporting is insufficient.** The catch block records only the exception message, not a reliable named stage, current URL/file, and preserved unique staging path. The log itself is created only after several directories have already been created.
5. **FFmpeg licensing conclusion is too broad if presented as legal verification.** The two build-flag checks are only a technical guard. Preserve build identity and upstream license/notice files, and state that this is not a complete legal audit.
6. **Manifest handling needs stricter validation.** It takes the first line containing the filename and does not reject ambiguous duplicate entries or validate that the parsed filename exactly equals the pinned asset name. v0.2 should require one unambiguous exact-name entry and log the source manifest URL and digest.
7. **No archive-content safety inventory.** Before extraction, v0.2 should inspect archive entries for unexpected absolute paths, parent traversal, or unexpected top-level layout; extraction must remain inside the unique staging directory.
8. **No complete final-payload acceptance report.** v0.2 should record exact versions, archive hashes, attestation result, executable paths, build configuration, preserved notice/license files, and final promotion result.

### Asset identity cross-check

The public FFmpeg-Builds release page confirms that tag `autobuild-2026-11-14-12-37` contains the exact pinned 9.0 build identity `n9.0.2-57-gd838f9f9ae` and the Windows x86_64 LGPL static archive name used by v0.1. This confirms the asset naming/release selection, not the downloaded archive bytes or a full licensing audit.

### Decision and next step

**Tool Setup v0.1 remains NO-GO. Do not execute it.** Prepare v0.2 as a separate script with unique staging under `PoC1`, strict path and archive-entry checks, exact manifest parsing, MediaMTX checksum plus GitHub Attestation verification, FFmpeg provenance/notice retention, stage-specific logs, and a no-overwrite same-volume promotion to `PoC1\\tools` only after all checks pass. A failure must preserve staging evidence and must not leave a partial final `tools` directory.

This tool preparation step does not authorize server startup, synthetic streams, live-edge tests, configuration edits, or any access to production or Rev.8.

**External source:** FFmpeg-Builds release listing, including the pinned 2026-11-14 12:37 asset: https://github.com/FFmpeg-Builds/FFmpeg-Builds/releases?expanded=true

## 2026-11-16 — MediaMTX v1.21.4 upstream release and issue review

**Scope and method:** Reviewed the official MediaMTX release and tag, tagged configuration and source, current `main`, the relevant gohlslib and mediacommon tagged sources, upstream issues/PRs, the published security-advisory list, and the MediaMTX license. This was an upstream-only review. No local installations, services, processes, configurations, recordings, or protected folders were accessed or changed. No implementation or architecture decision was made.

### Release status and exact baseline

- **Latest official release at review time: MediaMTX v1.21.4**, published 2026-11-15 13:33 UTC. The release points to signed, GitHub-verified commit `e65f9411a56b19076a0dfde1fb4a59414bdf642c`.
- The previously reviewed v1.21.3 candidate is no longer the latest release. Current `main` was only one commit ahead of v1.21.4 at review time; that commit (`42e79d0`) only moved documentation text.
- v1.21.4 updates the principal media dependencies from the v1.21.3 pins: gohlslib 2.4.7 → 2.4.8; gortmplib 1.0.5 → 1.0.6; gortsplib 5.6.7 → 5.7.0; mediacommon 2.9.7 → 2.10.0; Pion ICE 4.4.8 → 4.4.9; Pion WebRTC 4.2.24 → 4.2.26; gosrt 0.12.0 → 0.13.0.
- MediaMTX's bundled hls.js is also updated from v1.7.3 to v1.7.4. The official hls.js v1.7.4 release includes LL-HLS part-loading/playlist-position fixes and a fix for H.264 parameter-set truncation. This applies to MediaMTX's bundled web player; it does not prove that JudoReplayKit's own player asset is v1.7.4, and it does not fix the server-side PART-TARGET issue #6237.
- Pion WebRTC v4.2.26 is an official release (2026-10-28) containing a fix for a local-description SDP mutation race. This is a signaling/concurrency fix, not a change to RTP reader queues or media fan-out.
- MediaMTX pins the Pion ICE v4.4.9 tag, but no matching GitHub Release entry was found (the Pion ICE releases page still lists v4.4.8 as latest). The tagged v4.4.9 `udp_mux.go` has the same blob SHA as v4.4.8, so this dependency bump does not itself change the shared UDP-mux write path. The new MediaMTX `udpWriteBufferSize` setting is therefore the identified mitigation for #6100; no Pion ICE write-deadline fix was found in the pinned source.
- The v1.21.4 README still describes MediaMTX as a zero-dependency single executable compatible with Linux, Windows and macOS. No breaking OS-specific deployment change was identified in the release notes; the Windows #6468 RTSP report below is sparse and protocol-specific. The release workflow builds binaries and publishes checksums/attestations. Windows socket-buffer behavior remains untested in this review.
- The isolated PoC Tool Setup draft recorded earlier still pins v1.21.3. It was not edited as part of this review.

### Material released changes

#### 1. WebRTC UDP write-buffer control — issue #6100 closed by a released fix

- MediaMTX v1.21.4 adds the global `udpWriteBufferSize` setting through PR #6441. The upstream issue #6100 described a shared WebRTC ICE UDP-mux socket whose small OS-default send buffer could block writes and convoy unrelated ICE agents/readers; the reporter supplied macOS socket evidence and estimated similarly small Windows defaults.
- The v1.21.4 source applies a non-zero value to the shared WebRTC UDP mux socket before constructing the ICE UDP mux, and the WebRTC network wrapper applies it to other UDP sockets as well.
- **Important qualification:** the documented/default value is `0`, meaning “leave the operating-system default in place”. The release therefore adds a control; it does not automatically increase the buffer. The Windows default-size claim in #6100 is the reporter's observation/estimate, not a Windows reproduction verified in this review.
- This is a socket-buffer mitigation, not a change to MediaMTX's independent per-reader queues or proof of per-reader transport isolation. It should be measured with the actual Windows host, two camera paths, and the expected simultaneous readers before relying on it.

Sources:
- https://github.com/bluenviron/mediamtx/releases/tag/v1.21.4
- https://github.com/bluenviron/mediamtx/issues/6100
- https://github.com/bluenviron/mediamtx/pull/6441
- https://github.com/bluenviron/mediamtx/blob/v1.21.4/mediamtx.yml
- https://github.com/bluenviron/mediamtx/blob/v1.21.4/internal/servers/webrtc/server.go
- https://github.com/bluenviron/mediamtx/blob/v1.21.4/internal/protocols/webrtc/net.go

#### 2. WebRTC now respects the configured UDP payload ceiling

- PR #6464, included in v1.21.4, makes WebRTC packetization respect `udpMaxPayloadSize`. The documented default remains 1452 bytes; a lower value can be configured for low-MTU paths such as VPNs/tunnels.
- This is relevant to packet fragmentation/loss on constrained networks, but does not change the normal default or the reader-queue model. The local tournament LAN's actual MTU and any VPN path still need measurement.

Source: https://github.com/bluenviron/mediamtx/pull/6464

#### 3. H.265 fMP4 initialization metadata correction

- v1.21.4 updates mediacommon to v2.10.0. Its H.265 `hvcC` generation now removes emulation-prevention bytes from the SPS before extracting the 48-bit general-constraint indicator. The v2.9.7 code used by v1.21.3 did not perform this correction.
- The same class of correction is present in gortmplib v1.0.6's RTMP H.265 `hvcC` generator: v1.0.5 copied `sps[7:13]`, while v1.0.6 removes emulation-prevention bytes and reads RBSP bytes 8–13, also filling profile-space and tier fields. This is a separate RTMP writer/configuration path from mediacommon's MP4/fMP4 path.
- mediacommon v2.10.0 also fixes H.264 SPS width parsing when `ChromaArrayType = 0` (monochrome streams).
- These are released codec-container compatibility fixes. They do not establish that every camera's HEVC/H.264 profile, parameter-set arrangement, or browser decoder will work; actual camera SPS/PPS/VPS and generated init segments still require validation.

Sources:
- https://github.com/bluenviron/mediacommon/issues/404
- https://github.com/bluenviron/mediacommon/blob/v2.10.0/internal/mp4/codec_boxes.go
- https://github.com/bluenviron/gortmplib/pull/154
- https://github.com/bluenviron/gortmplib/blob/v1.0.6/writer.go
- https://github.com/bluenviron/mediamtx/releases/tag/v1.21.4
- https://github.com/video-dev/hls.js/releases/tag/v1.7.4
- https://github.com/pion/webrtc/releases/tag/v4.2.26
- https://github.com/pion/webrtc/commit/0e35f8059c5a7cb2002b53f8b2017f8148ee2fc7
- https://github.com/pion/ice/blob/v4.4.8/udp_mux.go
- https://github.com/pion/ice/blob/v4.4.9/udp_mux.go
- https://github.com/bluenviron/mediamtx/blob/v1.21.4/README.md
- https://github.com/bluenviron/mediamtx/blob/v1.21.4/.github/workflows/release.yml

#### 4. MPEG-TS timestamp behavior changed in mediacommon 2.10.0

- The MPEG-TS reader no longer rebases incoming timestamps to zero; it passes them through. This affects MPEG-TS/SRT inputs and may change the starting PTS/time origin observed by downstream consumers.
- gohlslib 2.4.8 separately restores zero-based rebasing for its HLS client after the mediacommon change, to keep HLS-source playback compatible. This is a source-specific distinction: it must not be generalized to RTMP or WebRTC input.
- For any JudoReplayKit path using SRT/MPEG-TS, test PTS origin, restart/discontinuity handling, and two-camera offset before interpreting timestamps as a common event clock.

Sources:
- https://github.com/bluenviron/mediacommon/issues/406
- https://github.com/bluenviron/mediacommon/blob/v2.10.0/pkg/formats/mpegts/reader.go
- https://github.com/bluenviron/gohlslib/pull/418
- https://github.com/bluenviron/mediamtx/releases/tag/v1.21.4

#### 5. Security hygiene and licensing

- v1.21.4 avoids writing arbitrary JSON request bodies (including Control API/authentication passwords and tokens) to debug logs, and redacts additional secrets and credentials from API output. This is a useful hardening change if API/debug logging is enabled.
- The currently published MediaMTX advisories reviewed have patched ranges below v1.21.4. In particular: the legacy playback-authorization issue GHSA-jh89-m3cv-fmv2 is patched from v1.21.2; the Control API CSRF issue GHSA-w334-5mp5-h897 from v1.21.1; and the zero-length RTMP H.264/H.265 parameter-set crash advisories from v1.21.0. The listed MoQ parsing/memory advisories are also patched in earlier releases; they remain relevant only if MoQ is exposed/used, but v1.21.4 is beyond their published affected ranges.
- MediaMTX's `LICENSE` file is byte-identical between v1.21.3 and v1.21.4 (same Git blob SHA); it remains MIT. This is not a complete audit of every transitive dependency's license obligations.

Sources:
- https://github.com/bluenviron/mediamtx/security/advisories
- https://github.com/bluenviron/mediamtx/security/advisories/GHSA-jh89-m3cv-fmv2
- https://github.com/bluenviron/mediamtx/security/advisories/GHSA-w334-5mp5-h897
- https://github.com/bluenviron/mediamtx/security/advisories/GHSA-5jgv-c722-r5cg
- https://github.com/bluenviron/mediamtx/security/advisories/GHSA-7498-vjgw-9cr2
- https://github.com/bluenviron/mediamtx/blob/v1.21.4/LICENSE

### Open upstream reports and unresolved risks

#### HIGH — recording-integrity risk: recorder time drift (#5550)

- Issue #5550 remains open and was updated with further reports through 2026-11-13. One user reported a 64-minute session in which the fMP4 archive covered only 2 minutes 14 seconds, with about 55 minutes during which no segment file was written after a drift reset. Other users independently reported repeated drift resets and late-sample discards on RTSP-camera/republisher paths, sometimes producing many short segments.
- The reports are user observations, not a maintainer-confirmed universal reproduction. Their proposed root causes differ, so they must not be collapsed into one proven explanation.
- The v1.21.4 recorder source still defines `ntpDriftTolerance = 5 seconds`. The fMP4 track code still resets when elapsed NTP and DTS diverge beyond that tolerance, discards samples whose DTS would produce a negative fMP4 base time, and selects the next segment start only among tracks within `maxBasetime = 1 second` of the most advanced track. These source facts make the reported failure mode important to test, but do not prove that every reset causes prolonged data loss.
- **JudoReplayKit consequence:** continuous live output is not evidence that the archive is complete. Test the actual direct camera publishing protocol and both cameras over long runs; compare wall-clock session duration, recorder logs, segment coverage, decoded frame count and audio/video timelines. Keep this as a high-priority recording-integrity gate until measured.

Sources:
- https://github.com/bluenviron/mediamtx/issues/5550
- https://github.com/bluenviron/mediamtx/blob/v1.21.4/internal/recorder/recorder.go
- https://github.com/bluenviron/mediamtx/blob/v1.21.4/internal/recorder/format_fmp4_track.go

#### MEDIUM — LL-HLS playlist stability issue remains unreleased (#6237 / gohlslib #398)

- MediaMTX issue #6237 remains open. It reports changing `PART-TARGET`/`PART-HOLD-BACK` values and a separate delta-playlist Skip-Boundary problem; the report includes an independent reproduction of the first defect.
- gohlslib PR #398 proposes to freeze `PART-TARGET` and move samples across part boundaries without dropping them, but the PR remains open. Tagged gohlslib v2.4.8 still contains the old part-target recomputation/mutation code, so this fix is **not** in the current MediaMTX release.
- The reported permanent playback failure is demonstrated for Apple CoreMedia/Safari and also describes hls.js audio-part errors. It is not proof that the custom MP4Box.js worker will fail in the same way. The project's existing plan to request a full playlist and avoid `_HLS_skip` avoids the reported delta-playlist path, but does not by itself fix changing part-target metadata.
- Treat this as an HLS-player compatibility gate, not as evidence against the separate WebRTC path.

Sources:
- https://github.com/bluenviron/mediamtx/issues/6237
- https://github.com/bluenviron/gohlslib/pull/398
- https://github.com/bluenviron/gohlslib/blob/v2.4.8/muxer_stream.go

#### LOW-CONFIDENCE WATCH — long-uptime Go runtime livelock report (#6454)

- Issue #6454 remains open and has no maintainer-confirmed reproduction in the material reviewed. The reporter describes one v1.21.2/Go 1.27.2 Linux deployment that, after about eight days, entered a roughly 9-hour-42-minute CPU livelock in Go's GC memory-limit accounting; all application I/O stopped. The reporter is also investigating possible host hardware/memory corruption.
- The impact would be severe if reproducible, but the evidence is a single incident and does not establish a MediaMTX defect or that v1.21.4 is affected. Keep it as a long-uptime observation item, not a confirmed blocker.

Source: https://github.com/bluenviron/mediamtx/issues/6454

#### Existing H.264 first-segment issue #6465 — status unchanged

- The previously logged placeholder-SPS report remains open. Direct source comparison confirms that v1.21.4 and current `main` still contain the fallback `h264DefaultSPS` when the track description lacks SPS/PPS, and the fMP4 init is written when the first part closes. No fix was found in v1.21.4.
- This is already documented in the 2026-11-15 entry “Additional MediaMTX H.264 compatibility gates: multiple PPS and fMP4 placeholder SPS”; this entry records only the status recheck, not a new finding.

Sources:
- https://github.com/bluenviron/mediamtx/issues/6465
- https://github.com/bluenviron/mediamtx/blob/v1.21.4/internal/recorder/format_fmp4.go
- https://github.com/bluenviron/mediamtx/blob/v1.21.4/internal/recorder/format_fmp4_segment.go

#### Low-confidence Windows-specific report (#6468)

- Issue #6468 reports RTSP playback stopping after 22 seconds with MediaMTX v1.21.3 on Windows amd64 and PotPlayer. It contains no logs or diagnostic evidence and remains open. It is specifically an RTSP/PotPlayer report, not the HTTP Playback or WebRTC/LL-HLS replay paths under review, so applicability to JudoReplayKit is presently low.

Source: https://github.com/bluenviron/mediamtx/issues/6468

### Areas rechecked with no new change in v1.21.4

- The v1.21.3 and v1.21.4 `internal/stream/reader.go` files have the same blob SHA, as do the WebRTC outbound-track files. No new per-reader queue/discard semantics, H.264 B-frame policy, late-reader GOP pre-roll, or reader-triggered keyframe-request change was found in this release.
- H.264 recovery-point SEI random-access handling and the H.265 random-access classification were already present in the v1.21.3 dependency set; they are not new v1.21.4 fixes.
- gohlslib v2.4.8's disk-backed `part_disk.go` has the same blob SHA as v2.4.7. The previously logged unsynchronized part-buffer/finalization race therefore remains unfixed in this release.
- The current MediaMTX license is unchanged MIT. The release continues to publish checksums and GitHub Attestations for its binaries.

### Assessment and safety status

- v1.21.4 is the current upstream release and contains useful WebRTC UDP-buffer, H.265 fMP4-header, timestamp-path and security-hygiene changes.
- The new UDP write-buffer setting is opt-in in practice because its default remains `0`; Windows multi-reader behavior is not runtime-proven.
- The most important unresolved upstream concern for JudoReplayKit remains recording integrity under timestamp drift (#5550), alongside the already logged first-segment SPS issue (#6465). The LL-HLS playlist issue is a separate browser/player compatibility risk.
- This review does **not** select v1.21.4 as the project's implementation baseline, change the Tool Setup draft, change configuration, or authorize a runtime test. The next relevant evidence must come from isolated PoCs using the actual camera protocol and target Windows environment.

## 2026-11-16 — Addendum: v0.1 checksum-manifest size-check defect and v0.2 draft correction

**Source:** Exact v0.1 PowerShell source and SHA-256 supplied by the user in the project conversation; local setup log and file inventory supplied by the user. This is a static source review, not an execution test.

### Confirmed v0.1 defect

The v0.1 helper `Download-File` applies this post-download condition to every file:

```powershell
if (-not (Test-Path -LiteralPath $Path) -or (Get-Item -LiteralPath $Path).Length -lt 1024) {
    throw "Download missing or unexpectedly small: $Path"
}
```

This treats every file smaller than 1,024 bytes as a failed download. The MediaMTX published checksum manifest in the user's local inventory is 715 bytes. The setup log records the manifest download attempt and STOP/ERROR at 16:09:54; the local manifest file's timestamp is also 16:09:54. The source-level defect therefore explains the reported stop: a successfully downloaded, valid small checksum manifest would still be rejected solely because of its size. This is a confirmed code defect; whether the specific local 715-byte file is authentic and complete remains unverified.

The minimum-size check is not a valid general-purpose download-integrity check. A corrected implementation must distinguish expected small text manifests from large archives and validate each artifact using its expected publisher checksum, release metadata, and/or other appropriate provenance evidence. A non-zero file-size check alone is insufficient.

### v0.1 disposition and remaining issues

- v0.1 remains **NO-GO**. Do not execute it.
- Its direct writes into the final tools directory, lack of staged/atomic promotion, lack of ZIP-entry safety inspection, and execution of downloaded binaries for version/build-configuration checks remain review concerns.
- Existing downloaded archives and manifests remain untrusted until independently verified. No extraction or execution is authorized by this addendum.

### Correction concerning the first v0.2 review draft

A first v0.2 draft was generated in a prior assistant tool session, but the file is not available in the current working container for inspection. The earlier assistant statement that no file had been created was incorrect; creation had succeeded in that prior tool session. However, the draft had a statically identified blocking defect: it called a `Download-File` helper that was not defined. It must not be used or run. The artifact must be recreated in the current session, reviewed, and explicitly delivered before it can be treated as an available review draft.

### Safety

No PowerShell script was run. No archive was extracted. No downloaded executable was started. No services, streams, firewall rules, configuration, registry, production installation, or Rev.8 installation were changed. This entry is an append-only clarification and does not authorize runtime activity.


## 2026-11-17 — Independent Gemini static review of Tool Setup v0.1

**Source:** The user supplied Gemini's full static-review response in the JudoReplayKit project conversation. The review was based on the exact v0.1 source pasted by the user, whose locally reported SHA-256 is `D815D8A37A0DC16F7522A31771FB61A3EA7E34BC3BE1713A13432F6B05C6BC9F`. No script execution or runtime testing was performed.

### Gemini's disposition

Gemini independently classifies Tool Setup v0.1 as **NO-GO** and recommends that it must not be run. It identifies the following main gaps:

- The generic 1,024-byte minimum-size check rejects the 715-byte MediaMTX checksum manifest.
- No staging directory or atomic promotion; the script writes directly into the final tools tree.
- No explicit ZIP-entry path validation before extraction.
- No attestation verification.
- No provenance manifest enumerating extracted files and hashes.
- Partial downloads and partially extracted output can remain after a failure.
- The script executes downloaded MediaMTX and FFmpeg binaries for version/build-configuration output.
- It relies on a hardcoded workspace path and has no second explicit guard against a future accidental path change.

These findings are consistent with the previously recorded v0.1 source review.

### Independent reconciliation and qualifications

The following qualifications are retained rather than adopting every statement in Gemini's response as established fact:

1. **PowerShell 5.1 download memory behavior:** Gemini states that `Invoke-WebRequest -OutFile` necessarily reads the complete download into RAM. That is too categorical and is not established by the supplied source alone. A v0.2 design should use a deliberate, bounded/streaming download implementation appropriate to Windows PowerShell 5.1, but must not describe whole-file RAM buffering as a proven defect without evidence.
2. **ZIP Slip:** The absence of explicit archive-entry validation is confirmed. However, the supplied evidence does not prove that the specific Windows PowerShell 5.1 `Expand-Archive` implementation in the target environment is exploitable. The design requirement remains: inspect every archive entry, reject rooted/traversal/invalid paths and links, enforce expansion limits, and extract only within a controlled destination.
3. **Cleanup versus forensic evidence:** Gemini recommends deleting the entire staging directory on failure. The project should not automatically destroy useful diagnostic evidence. A safer failure policy is to prevent promotion, retain a clearly isolated failure log and minimal metadata, and quarantine or remove untrusted archive bytes only under an explicit, documented policy. Any retained staging material must never be mistaken for installed/verified tools.
4. **Attestation scope:** MediaMTX GitHub Attestations should be verified against the expected repository/workflow identity if the release publishes a compatible attestation. This is an additional provenance check, not a replacement for digest verification. Do not assume the FFmpeg-Builds asset has the same attestation support; fail closed or document the distinct trust model.
5. **Protected-path guard:** A check that rejects every path on drive D: is broader than necessary and may block unrelated locations. Prefer a strict allowlist requiring the canonical resolved workspace to equal `C:\JudoReplayKit_Rev9_Dev`, combined with explicit rejection of the protected production and Rev.8 paths. Do not rely on a simple prefix match that could have path-boundary ambiguities.
6. **Provenance:** A v0.2 manifest should record the exact release/repository/tag/asset, publisher/API digests, verification outcomes, executable relative paths, and SHA-256 hashes of the final promoted files. A provenance file alone does not prove that the build is trustworthy.

### Consolidated v0.2 review requirements

Before any owner approval is considered, a new review draft must:

- Be explicitly pinned to the Rev.9 development workspace and refuse unexpected or protected paths after canonical path resolution.
- Refuse to overwrite an existing final tools directory or pre-existing staging content.
- Use unique staging and ensure promotion occurs only after all checks succeed.
- Implement a Windows PowerShell 5.1-compatible download method with explicit size bounds, expected sizes where known, and robust failure detection.
- Verify release metadata/API digests and exact publisher-manifest entries; reject ambiguous, duplicate, missing, or malformed checksum records.
- Verify MediaMTX attestation against the expected repository/workflow where supported; record the exact verification result and tool version. Do not claim an independent trust root when checks are controlled by the same publisher/platform.
- Inspect ZIP entries before extraction; reject absolute paths, drive/UNC paths, traversal components, empty/dot components, symbolic links and unsupported entry types; enforce entry-count and expanded-size limits; prevent duplicate/colliding destinations; verify every resolved target remains inside the extraction root.
- Avoid executing downloaded binaries during setup. Static PE architecture inspection may be used; runtime version checks belong in a separately approved test.
- Preserve license/notices and produce a final provenance/hash manifest.
- On failure, never promote partial content; preserve useful diagnostic evidence without leaving ambiguous partial installation state.
- Check native process exit codes where any external tool is used; avoid catch-handler behavior that masks the original failure.
- Include a clear preflight summary and explicit statement of every path and state it may modify.
- Receive independent static review of the exact delivered source and SHA-256 before any execution is considered.

### Current status

- v0.1 remains **NO-GO**.
- Gemini's review is an independent static review, not a runtime test.
- No v0.2 implementation is approved or authorized to run.
- The local PoC1 downloads remain unverified and must not be extracted or executed based solely on their presence or timestamps.
- No production, Rev.8, service, stream, firewall, configuration, registry, or PATH changes were authorized or performed in this review.

## 2026-11-17 — Tool Setup v0.3 review draft created after Gemini v0.2 review

**Source:** Assistant-authored revision of the delivered v0.2 REVIEW draft, incorporating the user's supplied Gemini static-review findings and an independent reconciliation. This is a source-only change; it has not been executed.

### Delivered artifact

- Filename: `JudoReplayKit_Rev9_PoC1_Tool_Setup_v0.3_REVIEW.ps1`
- SHA-256: `eb2a6fc46b62d3452f519a40cb2a6e95f7dd1c0a2a5f98a18edc66758a562434`
- Size: 27,934 bytes.
- Delivered as a standalone review draft. It is not approved to run.

### Changes from v0.2

- Added a narrowly scoped `Remove-OwnedStagingDirectory` cleanup function.
- The function only accepts a GUID-named `.staging-v0.3-<32 hex chars>` directory directly beneath the pinned Rev.9 tools directory.
- It refuses cleanup if the staging root is not a normal directory, if a reparse point is detected within staging, or if the canonical path is outside the expected tools directory.
- Cleanup is attempted in `finally` after both normal completion and catchable errors.
- After deletion, the script checks that the staging directory is actually absent.
- A cleanup failure is reported prominently. If there was no earlier setup error, cleanup failure causes the script to fail. If there was an earlier setup error, the original error is preserved and the cleanup failure is separately reported.
- v0.3 uses distinct download filenames and log names, avoiding overwrite of v0.1/v0.2 downloads and logs.
- Provenance schema and status text identify v0.3.

### Reconciliation of Gemini's claim

Gemini correctly identified that v0.2 retained staging material on ordinary catchable failures. The v0.3 change addresses that gap for normal completion and errors that PowerShell catches. It does **not** guarantee cleanup after power loss, OS crash, or forced process termination, because PowerShell's `finally` block is not guaranteed to execute in those circumstances. Reparse-point detection is intended to prevent recursive cleanup through unexpected links; a cleanup refusal is surfaced rather than silently ignored.

### Validation and limitations

- Source-level checks confirmed the v0.3 version identifiers, unique v0.3 download/log names, cleanup function, cleanup call in `finally`, and post-delete existence check.
- No Windows PowerShell 5.1 executable/parser is available in the authoring environment; PowerShell syntax parsing and runtime testing have **not** been performed.
- The script has not been run. No archives have been downloaded or extracted by it; no executable has been started; no services, streams, firewall, PATH, registry, Production, or Rev.8 have been modified.
- Gemini's GO for v0.2 does not transfer to v0.3. The exact v0.3 file and SHA-256 require a fresh independent static review.
- **Status: REVIEW DRAFT — NOT APPROVED TO RUN.**



## 2026-11-17 — Gemini independent static review of Tool Setup v0.3

**Source:** Full review text supplied by the user. Gemini reports a fresh independent static review of `JudoReplayKit_Rev9_PoC1_Tool_Setup_v0.3_REVIEW.ps1`, SHA-256 `eb2a6fc46b62d3452f519a40cb2a6e95f7dd1c0a2a5f98a18edc66758a562434`.

### Gemini's disposition

- Gemini assigns **GO** for isolated execution in `C:\JudoReplayKit_Rev9_Dev`.
- It approves the staging cleanup design, PowerShell 5.1 try/catch/finally behavior, workspace isolation, download checks, ZIP checks, attestation and provenance.
- Gemini correctly notes that `finally` cannot be guaranteed after process termination, power loss or OS crash.

### Independent artifact check and qualifications

- The uploaded v0.3 file was independently hashed in the authoring environment; its SHA-256 matches the exact hash reviewed by Gemini.
- The delivered file's actual line numbers do **not** match several line references in Gemini's report. For example, the cleanup function is at lines 326–359 (not 196–224), and the actual `finally` block is at lines 472–487 (not 319–335). The ZIP validation is at lines 217–267 (not 135–181). Gemini should be asked to confirm it reviewed the exact hash and explain the line-number discrepancy.
- The cleanup routine checks that the staging path is under the tools root, has the expected GUID leaf name, and contains no detected reparse points before recursive deletion. This is a meaningful safeguard. However, the prefix-plus-leaf checks alone do not mathematically prove that an arbitrary supplied path is a direct child; in this script `StageRoot` is internally constructed as a direct child. A concurrent local process changing the directory between validation and deletion is also outside what these checks fully prevent.
- On download failures, a partial `.part` file can remain because the download helper does not remove it. It is not promoted or extracted, and later runs refuse to overwrite it; manual inspection/removal may be needed. This is safe against silent reuse but is not automatic cleanup of every temporary artifact.
- The script still has not been parsed or executed under Windows PowerShell 5.1 in the target environment. Static review does not establish runtime success, download success, attestation success, archive extraction success, or tool operability.

### Current decision

Gemini's GO is recorded as an independent static-review opinion, not as owner authorization to execute. The exact delivered v0.3 source remains subject to the project's explicit execution approval and a bounded execution plan. Until that approval is given, do not run it. Production and Rev.8 remain protected.
